is a standard orthonormal basis and
p_{\sigma}
is a real multilinear polynomial for all
\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}
, the noise operator
\Gamma_{\gamma}
is defined to be

\Gamma_{\gamma}\left(P(\mathbf{x})\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}}\left(T_{\gamma}p_{\sigma}\right)\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\Delta_{\gamma}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right).

The lemma below follows directly from
Definition

3.2
and
2.12
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Given
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
,
h,n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m\geq 2
, an
(m^{2},n)
ensemble
\mathbf{x}
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}}p_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is a standard orthonormal basis and
p_{\sigma}
is a real multilinear polynomial for all
\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}
, suppose that for all
\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}
,
p_{\sigma}
has an expansion

p_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\tau\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{p_{\sigma}}(\tau)\mathbf{x}_{\tau}.

It holds that

\Gamma_{\gamma}\left(P(\mathbf{x})\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}}\sum_{\tau\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\gamma^{\left|\sigma\right|+\left|\tau\right|}\widehat{p_{\sigma}}(\tau)\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

(12)

We need a hypercontractivity inequality for Hermitian matrices.

Fact 3.8
.

[
QY21
, Lemma 8.3]

Given
h,n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m\geq 2
,
0\leq\gamma\leq\left(9m\right)^{-1/4}
and
P\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, it holds that

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Delta_{\gamma}^{\otimes n}\left(P\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2},

where
\Delta_{\gamma}\left(\cdot\right)
is defined in
Definition

2.11
.

The main result in this subsection is stated below.

Theorem 3.9

(Hypercontractivity for random matrices)
.

Given
h,n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m\geq 2
,
0<\eta<1
,
0\leq\gamma\leq\min\left\{\eta,\left(9m\right)^{-1/4}\right\}
, a
(2,4,\eta)
-hypercontractive
(m^{2},n)
ensemble
\mathbf{x}
and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}p_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is a standard orthonormal basis, and
p_{\sigma}
is a real multilinear polynomial for all
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}
, it holds that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Gamma_{\gamma}\left(P(\mathbf{x})\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\leq\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2},

where
\Gamma_{\gamma}
is defined in
Definition

3.6
.

Proof.

Set
Q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}\left(T_{\gamma}p_{\sigma}\right)\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}
. Then by the definition of
\Gamma_{\gamma}
,

\Gamma_{\gamma}\left(P(\mathbf{x})\right)=\Delta_{\gamma}\left(Q(\mathbf{x})\right).

Using
3.8
,

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Delta_{\gamma}\left(Q(\mathbf{x})\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\leq\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{4}\right].

(13)

Denote
q_{\sigma}=T_{\gamma}p_{\sigma}
. Notice that

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{4}\right]=~

\displaystyle m^{-2h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left(\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}q_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}\right]\leq m^{-2h}\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}p_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{2}=\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2},

where the inequality follows from
3.4
and
Lemma

3.5
. We conclude the result by combining it with
Eq.

13
.

∎

The following is an application of
Theorem

3.9
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Given
h,n,m,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m\geq 2
,
0<\eta<1
, a
(2,4,\eta)
-hypercontractive
(m^{2},n)
ensemble
\mathbf{x}
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}}p_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is a standard orthonormal basis and for all
\sigma\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{h}
and
p_{\sigma}
is a real multilinear polynomial satisfying
\deg\left(p_{\sigma}\right)+\left|\sigma\right|\leq d
, it holds that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\leq\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2}.

Proof.

Suppose that for all
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}
,
p_{\sigma}
has an expansion

p_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\tau\in\left[m^{2}\right]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{p_{\sigma}}(\tau)\mathbf{x}_{\tau}.

Set

P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\sigma\in[m^2]_{\geq 0}^h,\tau\in\Br{m^2}_{\geq 0}^n :}{\ab{\sigma}+\ab{\tau}=i}}\widehat{p_{\sigma}}\left(\tau\right)\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

Set
\gamma=\min\left\{\eta,\left(9m\right)^{-1/4}\right\}
. Applying
Lemma

3.7
and
Theorem

3.9
,

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Gamma_{\gamma}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\gamma^{-i}P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\leq\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\gamma^{-i}P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}\right]\right)^{2}

By the orthogonality of
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathcal{B}
, if
i\neq j
, we have

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})P^{=j}(\mathbf{x})\right]=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\gamma^{-2i}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}\right]\right)^{2}\leq\gamma^{-4d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{=i}(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}\right]\right)^{2}=\gamma^{-4d}\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{x})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2}.

∎

3.2
Invariance principle

We are now prepared to introduce an invariance principle on matrix space applicable to general functions. Initially, we establish the proof for functions in
\mathcal{C}^{4}
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Given
0<\tau,\eta<1
,
d,h,m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
H\subseteq[n]
of size
\left|H\right|=h
,
\xi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
satisfying
\lVert\xi^{(3)}\rVert_{\infty}\leq B
where
B
is a constant, and a
(2,4,\eta)
-hypercontractive
(m^{2},n)
ensemble
\mathbf{x}
, let
P\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
be a degree-
d
operator satisfying
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)\leq\tau
for all
i\notin H
. Suppose that
P
has a Fourier expansion

P=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

Let

P^{H}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{\overline{H}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}}.

If
\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\leq 1
, we have

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq 2c_{3}B\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d

for some absolute constant
c_{3}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\overline{H}=[n-h]
. We prove this by a hybrid argument. For any
0\leq i\leq n-h
, define the hybrid basis elements and the hybrid random operators as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i\right)}=\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{\leq i}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{>i}}~\mbox{for $\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}$};

(14)

\displaystyle P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]^{n}_{\geq 0}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}^{\left(i\right)}_{\sigma},

(15)

where
\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{\leq i}}=\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{i}}

and
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{>i}}=\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{i+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{n}}
.
Then
P=P^{(0)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)
and
P^{H}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=P^{(n-h)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)
. Note that

\displaystyle P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}=0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i\right)}+\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i\right)},

\displaystyle P^{\left(i+1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}=0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i+1\right)}+\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i+1\right)},

Set

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}=0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i\right)};\quad\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i\right)};

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}=0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i+1\right)};\quad\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}^{\left(i+1\right)}.

Then we have

\displaystyle P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B};~P^{\left(i+1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\mathbf{C}+\mathbf{D}.

Notice that
\mathbf{A}=\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{C}
, where
\mathds{1}_{m}
is placed in the
(i+1)
-th register. Thus,

\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{A}\right)=m\cdot\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{C}\right).

(16)

From
2.18
and then
Eq.

16
,

\displaystyle\left|m^{i+1-n}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i+1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\right]-m^{i-n}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left|\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{ m^{i+1-n}\br{\Tr~\xi\br{C}+\Tr~D\xi\br{\mathbf{C}}\Br{\mathbf{D}}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr~D^2\xi\br{\mathbf{C}}\Br{\mathbf{D}}+\Delta_{3,\xi}(\randC,\randD)}-}{ m^{i-n}\br{\Tr~\xi\br{A}+\Tr~D\xi\br{\mathbf{A}}\Br{\mathbf{B}}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr~D^2\xi\br{\mathbf{A}}\Br{\mathbf{B}}+\Delta_{3,\xi}(\randA,\randB)}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left|\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{ m^{i+1-n}\br{\Tr~D\xi\br{\mathbf{C}}\Br{\mathbf{D}}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr~D^2\xi\br{\mathbf{C}}\Br{\mathbf{D}}+\Delta_{3,\xi}(\randC,\randD)}-}{ m^{i-n}\br{\Tr~D\xi\br{\mathbf{A}}\Br{\mathbf{B}}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr~D^2\xi\br{\mathbf{A}}\Br{\mathbf{B}}+\Delta_{3,\xi}(\randA,\randB)}}\right]\right|

Both the first-order and second-order derivatives cancel out because of the following claim.

Claim 3.12
.

It holds that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D\xi\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\left[\mathbf{B}\right]\right]=m\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D\xi\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\left[\mathbf{D}\right]\right];

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{2}\xi\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\left[\mathbf{B}\right]\right]=m\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{2}\xi\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\left[\mathbf{D}\right]\right].

By
2.18
, there exists a universal constant
c_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[m^{i+1-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i+1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)-m^{i-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle c_{3}B\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{3}^{3}\right]+\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{3}^{3}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle c_{3}B\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{2}\right]+\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{2}\right]\right)\quad\mbox{(H\"{o}lder's)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle c_{3}B\left(\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\right)^{1/2}+\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\right)^{1/2}\right)\quad\mbox{(Cauchy-Schwartz)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle c_{3}B\theta^{d}\left(\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{3/2}+\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{3/2}\right)\quad\mbox{(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:hybridHC})},

where
\theta=\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}
. Notice that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i+1}\neq 0}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\right|=\mathrm{Inf}_{i+1}\left(P\right).

Therefore,

\left|\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[m^{i+1-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i+1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)-m^{i-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{\left(i\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\right]\right|\leq 2c_{3}B\theta^{d}\mathrm{Inf}_{i+1}\left(P\right)^{3/2}.

Summing over
i\in[n-h]_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2c_{3}B\theta^{d}\sum_{i\notin H}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2c_{3}B\theta^{d}\sqrt{\tau}\sum_{i\notin H}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2c_{3}B\theta^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2c_{3}B\theta^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d.

∎

It remains to prove
3.12
.

Proof of
3.12
.

Note that
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{C};\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{B}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\otimes\mathbf{X}_{\sigma};\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{D}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}

for some random matrices
\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}
’s which are independent of
\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}
’s, where
\mathds{1}_{m}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\sigma}
’s are in the
\left(i+1\right)
-th register.

Suppose that
\mathbf{C}
has a spectral decomposition

\mathbf{C}=\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{\Pi}_{j},

where
m^{\prime}
is the dimension of
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{a}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\mathbf{a}_{m^{\prime}}
,
\left\{\Pi_{j}\right\}_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}
are rank-one projectors satisfying that
\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}\Pi_{j}=\mathds{1}
and
\Pi_{j}\Pi_{k}=0
for all
j\neq k
.

By
2.17
, we have

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D\xi\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\left[\mathbf{B}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[1]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\right)\mathbf{B}\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[1]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\right)\mathbf{B}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\right)\mathbf{B}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\mathbf{B}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\left(\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\right)\left(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\otimes\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}~\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\right]=0,

where the last equality follows from the orthogonality of
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
.

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D\xi\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\left[\mathbf{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\mathbf{D}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}~\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}:\sigma\neq 0}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}\right]\cdot\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 0,

where the last equality follows from the orthogonality of
\mathbf{x}
.

By
2.17
, we have

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{2}\xi\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\left[\mathbf{B}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\right)\mathbf{B}\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\right)\mathbf{B}\left(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma,\tau\neq 0}\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{X}_{\tau}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right],

where the last equality follows from the orthogonality of
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
.

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{2}\xi\left(\mathbf{C}\right)\left[\mathbf{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma,\tau\neq 0}\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\tau}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{X}_{\tau}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma,\tau\neq 0}\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\sigma}\mathbf{x}_{i+1,\tau}\right]\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{X}_{\tau}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\sum_{j,k,\ell\in[m^{\prime}]}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\xi^{[2]}\left(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{k}\mathbf{X}_{\sigma}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\ell}\right)\right],

where the last equality follows from the orthogonality of
\mathbf{x}
.
∎

For those functions that are not sufficiently smooth, if they have a mollifier, which is a smooth approximator with a bounded third derivative, then the invariance principle still holds. The following lemma proves an invariance principle for
\zeta\left(\cdot\right)
defined in
Eq.

8
, which has a mollifier
\zeta_{\lambda}\left(\cdot\right)
guaranteed by
2.21
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Given
0<\tau,\eta<1
,
d,h,m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
H\subseteq[n]
of size
\left|H\right|=h
, a
(2,4,\eta)
-hypercontractive
(m^{2},n)
ensemble
\mathbf{x}
and a degree-
d

P\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
satisfying
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)\leq\tau
for all
i\notin H
. Suppose that
P
has a Fourier expansion

P=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

Let

P^{H}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathbf{x}_{\sigma_{\overline{H}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}}.

If
\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\leq 1
, we have

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq C\left(\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d\right)^{2/3}

for some universal constants
C
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda>0
be determined later, and
\zeta_{\lambda}
be defined as in
2.21
. By
Theorem

3.11
and
2.21
,

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq 2c_{3}B_{3}\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d/\lambda,

where
c_{3},B_{3}
are universal constants. By
2.21
we also have

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P\right)-m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P\right)\right|\leq 2\lambda^{2}

and

\left|m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq 2\lambda^{2}.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq 4\lambda^{2}+2c_{3}B_{3}\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d/\lambda.

Choosing
\lambda=\left(2c_{3}B_{3}\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d/8\right)^{1/3},
we have

\left|m^{-n}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P\right)-m^{-h}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P^{H}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\right|\leq 3\left(2c_{3}B_{3}\max\left\{9m,1/\eta^{4}\right\}^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d\right)^{2/3}.

Let
C=3\left(2c_{3}B_{3}\right)^{2/3}
, we conclude the result.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

It is possible to prove an invariance principle for a broader class of functions. For example, we can prove it for Lipschitz continuous functions using the argument in
[
IM12
, Lemma 3.5]
. However, it is out of the focus of this paper. We will leave it for further research.

3.3
Derandomized invariance principle

From
Theorem

3.11
, it is not hard to see that the non-identity basis elements can be substituted by independent Rademacher variables. In this section, we will replace those Rademacher variables with pseudorandom variables to save the randomness. It is worth noting that there is a large body of research on derandomization through invariance principles (readers may refer to
[
OST22
]
and the references therein). We adopt the pseudorandom generator (PRG) introduced in
[
MZ10
]
. The PRG is constructed by pairwise uniform hash functions as follows.

For
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f:[n]\to[p]\right\}
,
define
G:\mathcal{F}\times\left(\left\{-1,1\right\}^{n}\right)^{p}\to\left\{-1,1\right\}^{n}
by

G\left(f,z^{1},\dots,z^{p}\right)=x\text{, where }x_{i}=z^{f(i)}_{i}\text{ for }i\in[n].

(17)

We define the influence of a random variable in a random matrix using the notation
\mathrm{VarInf}\left(\cdot\right)
to distinguish from the notation for the influence of a register in
Definition

2.8
.

Definition 3.15
.

Given
m,n,p\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, let
P(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\mathbf{b}_{S}P_{S}
be a random matrix with
\mathbf{b}
drawn uniformly from
\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, where
P_{S}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
and
\mathbf{b}_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\mathbf{b}_{i}
for all
S\subseteq[n]
. Then the influence of
i
’th coordinate of
\mathbf{b}
is defined to be

\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)=\sum_{S\ni i}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}.

We also define the influence of a block of coordinates.
Let
j\in[p]
and
f:[n]\to[p]
be a function, define the influence on the block
f^{-1}(j)\subseteq[n]
to be

\mathrm{VarInf}_{f,j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}.

The following is the main theorem in this section.

Theorem 3.16

(Derandomized invariance principle for
\zeta
)
.

Given
d,h,m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
m>1
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\mathbf{b}_{S}P_{S},

where
\mathbf{b}\sim_{\mathrm{u}}\left\{-1,1\right\}^{n}
,
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 1
,
\mathbf{b}_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\mathbf{b}_{i}
and
P_{S}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes h}
, they satisfy
\left|S\right|+\deg\left(P_{S}\right)\leq d
and
\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\leq\tau
for all
i\in[n]
.

Let
p
be the smallest power of
2
satisfying
p\geq d/\tau
;
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f:[n]\to[p]\right\}
be a family of pairwise uniform hash functions. For any
i\in[p]
, define
\mathbf{z}^{i}
to be a
4d
-wise uniform random vector drawn from
\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{z}^{i}
are independent across
i\in[p]
. Given
f\in\mathcal{F}
, denote
\mathbf{x}_{f}=G\left(f,\mathbf{z}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{p}\right)
as in
Eq.

17
. Then we have

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq C_{2}\sqrt{(9m)^{d}d\tau},

where
\mathbf{f}
is drawn uniformly from
\mathcal{F}
and
C_{2}
is a universal constant.

We first prove a derandomized invariance principle for the functions with bounded fourth derivative.

Theorem 3.17

(Derandomized invariance principle)
.

Given
d,h,m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
m>1
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\mathbf{b}_{S}P_{S},

where
\mathbf{b}\sim_{\mathrm{u}}\left\{-1,1\right\}^{n}
,
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 1
,
\mathbf{b}_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\mathbf{b}_{i}
and
P_{S}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes h}
, they satisfy that
\left|S\right|+\deg\left(P_{S}\right)\leq d
and
\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\leq\tau
for all
i\in[n]
.

Let
p
be the smallest power of
2
satisfying
p\geq d/\tau
;
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f:[n]\to[p]\right\}
be a family of pairwise uniform hash functions. For any
i\in[p]
, define
\mathbf{z}^{i}
to be a
4d
-wise uniform random vector drawn from
\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{z}^{i}
are independent across
i\in[p]
. Given
f\in\mathcal{F}
, denote
\mathbf{x}_{f}=G\left(f,\mathbf{z}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{p}\right)
as in
Eq.

17
. Then for any
\xi\in\mathcal{C}^{4}
with
\lVert\xi^{(4)}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{0}
where
C_{0}
is a constant, it holds that

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq 4C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}d\tau,

where
\mathbf{f}
is drawn uniformly from
\mathcal{F}
and
C_{1}
is a universal constant.

Assuming
Theorem

3.17
,
Theorem

3.16
is straightforward:

Proof of
Theorem

3.16
.

Let
\lambda>0
be determined later and let
\zeta_{\lambda}
be defined as in
2.21
. By
Theorem

3.17
and
2.21
,

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq 4C_{1}B_{4}\lambda^{-2}(9m)^{d}d\tau,

where
C_{1},B_{4}
are universal constants. By
2.21
we also have

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]\right|\leq 2\lambda^{2}

and

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq 2\lambda^{2}.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq 4\lambda^{2}+4C_{1}B_{4}\lambda^{-2}(9m)^{d}d\tau.

Choosing
\lambda=\left(C_{1}B_{4}(9m)^{d}d\tau\right)^{1/4}
, we have

\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|\leq 8\left(C_{1}B_{4}(9m)^{d}d\tau\right)^{1/2}.

Let
C_{2}=8\sqrt{C_{1}B_{4}}
, we conclude the result.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

It is also possible to generalize
Theorem

3.16
to Lipschitz continuous functions using the argument in
[
IM12
, Lemma 3.5]
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Given
d,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:\left|S\right|\leq d}\mathbf{b}_{S}P_{S},

where
\mathbf{b}
is a
2d
-wise uniform random vector from
\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
and
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 1
, it holds that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\leq d.

Proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{S\ni i}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:\left|S\right|\leq d}\left|S\right|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle d\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:\left|S\right|\leq d}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle d\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\quad\leq d.

∎

The following lemma is crucial to our proof. The proof follows closely to the proof of
[
MZ10
, Lemma 5.4]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Given
d,n,p\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, and a random matrix

P(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:\left|S\right|\leq d}\mathbf{b}_{S}P_{S},

satisfying
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P(\mathbf{b})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 1
,
where
\mathbf{b}
is a
2d
-wise uniform random vector drawn from
\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, let
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f:[n]\to[p]\right\}
be a family of pairwise uniform hash functions. Then for
\mathbf{f}\sim_{\mathrm{u}}\mathcal{F}
,

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathrm{VarInf}_{\mathbf{f},j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{p}.

Proof.

Fix
j\in[p]
and for
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathbf{X}_{i}
be the indicator variable that is 1 if
f(i)=j
and 0 otherwise. For brevity, let
\tau_{i}=\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)
for
i\in[n]
. Now,

\displaystyle\mathrm{VarInf}_{\mathbf{f},j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)=~

\displaystyle\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{S}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\left(\sum_{i\in S}\mathbf{X}_{i}\right)=\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{X}_{i}\sum_{S\ni i}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{X}_{i}\tau_{i}

Thus

\mathrm{VarInf}_{\mathbf{f},j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{X}_{i}\tau_{i}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{2}\tau_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq k}\mathbf{X}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{k}\tau_{i}\tau_{k}.

Note that
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathbf{X}_{i}\right]=1/p
and for
i\neq k
,
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathbf{X}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{k}\right]=1/p^{2}
. Thus

\displaystyle\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{VarInf}_{\mathbf{f},j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}\right]\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\tau_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq k}\tau_{i}\tau_{k}\frac{1}{p^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i}\tau_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left(\sum_{i}\tau_{i}\right)^{2}.

The lemma follows by using
Lemma

3.19
and summing all
j\in[p].

∎

We are ready to prove
Theorem

3.17
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.17
.

We prove this by a hybrid argument.
Denote
\mathbf{b}^{(0)}=\mathbf{b}=G\left(f,\mathbf{b},\dots,\mathbf{b}\right)
.
For
j\in[p]
, define
\mathbf{b}^{(j)}=G\left(f,\mathbf{z}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{j},\mathbf{b},\dots,\mathbf{b}\right)
,
i.e., substituting
\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}|_{f^{-1}\left(j\right)}
with
\mathbf{z}^{j}_{f^{-1}\left(j\right)}
. Then
\mathbf{b}^{(p)}=\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)=\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}_{S}P_{S}+\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}_{S}P_{S}

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)=\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}_{S}P_{S}+\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}_{S}P_{S}.

Note that for
S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)=\emptyset
,
\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}_{S}=\mathbf{b}^{(j)}_{S}
. Denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)=\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}_{S}P_{S},\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{B}=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}_{S}P_{S},\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{C}=\sum_{S:S\cap f^{-1}\left(j\right)\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}_{S}P_{S}.

We have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j)})\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{C}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{k=0}^{3}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{Tr}~D^{k}\xi(\mathbf{A})[\mathbf{B}]+\mathrm{Tr}~\Delta_{4,\xi}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{k=0}^{3}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{Tr}~D^{k}\xi(\mathbf{A})[\mathbf{C}]+\mathrm{Tr}~\Delta_{4,\xi}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})\right]\right|

By
2.17
and the fact that
\mathbf{z}_{j}
is
4d
-wise uniform, we have for
k=0,1,2,3
,

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{k}\xi(\mathbf{A})\left[\mathbf{B}\right]\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~D^{k}\xi(\mathbf{A})\left[\mathbf{C}\right]\right].

Thus,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j)})\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left|\mathrm{Tr}~\Delta_{4,\xi}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\right|\right]+\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left|\mathrm{Tr}~\Delta_{4,\xi}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C_{1}C_{0}\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]+\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\right),

where the last inequality is from
2.18
, and
C_{1}
is a universal constant. Because
\mathbf{z}_{j}
is
4d
-wise uniform, we have
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]
. Using
Theorem

3.10
with
\eta\leftarrow 1/\sqrt{3}
Recall that
\mathbf{b}
is
(2,4,1/\sqrt{3})
-hypercontractive,

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{4}^{4}\right]\leq(9m)^{d}\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2}.

So we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{b}^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\xi\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{b}^{(j)})\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}^{(j-1)}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{VarInf}_{\mathbf{f},j}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}\right].

Summing over
j\in[p]
and by
Lemma

3.20
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]-\frac{1}{m^{h}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{f}})\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}\left(\tau\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{VarInf}_{i}\left(P(\mathbf{b})\right)+\frac{d^{2}}{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 4C_{1}C_{0}(9m)^{d}d\tau,

where the last inequality is by
Lemma

3.19
and
p\geq d/\tau
.
∎

4
Positivity tester for low degree operators

In this section, we will present an algorithm deciding whether a low-degree operator is
\left(\beta-\delta\right)
-close to a positive semidefinite operator or
\left(\beta+\delta\right)
-far from all positive semidefinite operators,
for error parameters
\beta>\delta>0
.
The input operator is given in the form of a Fourier expansion.

Definition 4.1

(Positivity testing problem)
.

Given
d,{D},m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m>1
, and real numbers
\beta>\delta>0
, the input is a degree-
d
operator in
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes{D}}
given in the form of Fourier expansion

P=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{D}\\
\sigma:\lvert\sigma\rvert\leq d\end{subarray}}\widehat{P}(\sigma)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

Distinguish the following two cases.

•

Yes: if
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)<\beta-\delta
.

•

No: if
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)>\beta+\delta
.

Notice that the number of Fourier coefficients is
\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\binom{{D}}{i}}\left(m^{2}-1\right)^{i}
. If we are concerned with constant-degree operators, then the dimension of the operator is exponential in the input size.

Theorem 4.2
.

Given
d,{D},m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},

m>1
, and real numbers
\beta>\delta>0
,
there exists a deterministic algorithm for the positivity testing problem that runs in time

\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}\left(m^{d},1/\delta\right)\right)\cdot{D}^{O(d)}.

In particular, if
m,d,\delta
are constants, then the algorithm runs in time
\operatorname{poly}({D})
.

4.1
Algorithm

The algorithm is shown in
Fig.

1
, which applies the invariance principle
Lemma

3.13
to reduce the dimension of the matrices and then
Theorem

3.16
to derandomize, while the distance to positive operators is approximately preserved.

Input:

Parameters given in
Definition

4.1
.

Algorithm:

Perform the following steps

1.

Regularization
:
Let

\tau=\frac{\delta^{3}}{C^{\prime}\cdot(9m)^{2{d}}\cdot{d}^{2}},

(18)

where
C^{\prime}=\max\{8C^{3},4C_{2}^{2}\}
, with
C
and
C_{2}
originating from
Lemma

3.13
and
Theorem

3.16
, respectively.

For each
i
, compute the influence

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i}\neq 0}\widehat{P}(\sigma)^{2}
.
Let
H=\left\{i:\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)>\tau\right\}
.

2.

Derandomized invariance principle
:
Let
p
be the smallest power of
2
satisfying
p\geq{d}/\tau
.
Let
n=(m^{2}-1)\left({D}-\lvert H\rvert\right)
and
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f:[n]\to[p]\right\}
be a family of pairwise uniform hash functions.
For any
i\in[p]
, let
\mathbf{z}^{i}
be
4d
-wise uniform random variables of length
n
and
\left(\mathbf{z}^{i}\right)
’s be independent across
i\in[p]
.
For any
f\in\mathcal{F}
, set
\mathbf{x}_{f}=G\left(f,\mathbf{z}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{p}\right)
as defined in
Theorem

3.16
. Define the random operator

P^{\prime}(f,\mathbf{z})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{{D}}:\left|\sigma\right|\leq d}\widehat{P}(\sigma)\mathbf{x}_{f,\sigma_{\bar{H}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}},

(19)

where
\mathbf{x}_{f,\sigma_{\bar{H}}}=\prod_{i\notin H}\left(\mathbf{x}_{f}\right)_{(m^{2}-1)(i-1)+\sigma_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}}=\bigotimes_{i\in H}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{i}}
.

3.

Compute the distance to PSD: For each
f,\mathbf{z}
, compute

\delta_{f,\mathbf{z}}={m^{-\lvert H\rvert}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{\prime}(f,\mathbf{z})).

4.

Accept if

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}f,\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\delta_{f,\mathbf{z}}\right]<\beta.

Figure 1:
Positivity testing algorithm

4.2
Time complexity

1.

Given that each computation of
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)
entails calculating a sum of products of Fourier coefficients, the time required can be expressed as
\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\binom{{D}}{i}}\left(m^{2}-1\right)^{i}\leq dm^{2d}{D}^{d}
. In addition, the time needed to determine the set
H
is at most
{D}
.

2.

When fixing
f
and
\mathbf{z}
, computing
\delta_{f,\mathbf{z}}
takes time

\exp\left(\left|H\right|\right)=\exp\left(\left|d/\tau\right|\right)=\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}\left(m^{d},1/\delta\right)\right).

3.

By
Lemma

2.24
and
2.25
, the enumeration over
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbf{z}
takes time polynomial in
{D}
, thus computing the expectation of
\delta_{f,\mathbf{z}}
also takes time polynomial in
{D}
.

4.3
Correctness

Now we proceed to the correctness proof.
The first step in our algorithm is to use
Lemma

3.13
to reduce the dimension by introducing
Rademacher random variables.
Let
\mathbf{b}\in\left\{-1,1\right\}^{n}
be uniformly distributed.
Consider the operator
P^{(1)}
obtained by replacing the basis outside of
H
with random bits.
That is,

P^{(1)}(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{{D}}:\left|\sigma\right|\leq d}\widehat{P}(\sigma)\mathbf{b}_{\sigma_{\bar{H}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}},

where
\mathbf{b}_{\sigma_{\bar{H}}}=\prod_{i\notin H}\mathbf{b}_{(m^{2}-1)(i-1)+\sigma_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}}=\bigotimes_{i\in H}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{i}}
.
Recall that
\mathbf{b}
is
(2,4,1/\sqrt{3})
-hypercontractive,
so in
Lemma

3.13
we have
1/\eta^{4}=9\leq 9m
. By our choice of
\tau
, the right hand side of the bound in
Lemma

3.13
can be upper bounded as

C\left((9m)^{d}\sqrt{\tau}d\right)^{2/3}\leq{\delta}/2.

(20)

This implies

\left|\frac{1}{m^{\lvert H\rvert}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(1)}(\mathbf{b}))\right]-\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)\right|\leq\delta/2.

The second step is derandomization by
Theorem

3.16
.
We define
P^{(2)}
to be the operator obtained by replacing
\mathbf{b}
with
\mathbf{x}_{f,\mathbf{z}}
,
which is the operator in
Eq.

19
.
Then the right hand side of the bound in
Theorem

3.16
can be upper bounded as

C_{2}\sqrt{(9m)^{d}{d}\tau}\leq\delta/2.

(21)

By
Theorem

3.16
this implies

\left|\frac{1}{m^{\lvert H\rvert}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(2)}(\mathbf{x}_{f,\mathbf{z}}))\right]-\frac{1}{m^{\lvert H\rvert}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}f,\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(1)}(\mathbf{b}))\right]\right|\leq\delta/2.

Thus by triangle inequality, we have

\left|\frac{1}{m^{\lvert H\rvert}}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}f,\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(2)}(\mathbf{x}_{f,\mathbf{z}}))\right]-\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)\right|\leq\delta.

(22)

The algorithm computes
m^{-\lvert H\rvert}\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}f,\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(2)}(\mathbf{x}_{f,\mathbf{z}}))\right]
. By Eq.(
22
), the value is smaller than
\beta
if
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)<\beta-\delta
; or greater than
\beta
if
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P)>\beta+\delta
. Therefore, the algorithm distinguishes the two cases correctly.

5
Noisy nonlocal games are NP-complete

Definition 5.1

(Noisy Nonlocal Game Value Problem)
.

The input consists of the description of a nonlocal game,
which is a tuple
\mathfrak{G}=\left(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mu,V\right)
,
and real values
\rho,\beta
and
\varepsilon
.

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are question sets and assume
\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert=\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert=s
.

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are answer sets and assume
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert=\lvert\mathcal{B}\rvert=t
.
Let
\mu
be a distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and
V:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
be the predicate.

Let
v=\mathrm{val}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{G},\psi_{AB})
be the value of the nonlocal game,
where Alice and Bob share arbitrarily many copies of a noisy MES
\psi_{AB}
with the quantum maximal correlation
\rho
.
Let
1>\beta>\varepsilon>0
. The task is to distinguish the following two cases.

•

Yes:
v>\beta+\varepsilon
.

•

No:
v<\beta-\varepsilon
.

In this section, we show:

Theorem 5.2
.

The noisy nonlocal game value problem is
\mathrm{NP}
-complete.

It follows from the two propositions below.

Proposition 5.3
.

There exists a nondeterministic algorithm that runs in time

\operatorname{poly}\left(s,\operatorname{eexp}\left(t,\log\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\right),\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

that solves the noisy nonlocal game value problem.
Here
\operatorname{eexp}(\cdot)
means doubly exponential.
In particular, if
t,\rho,\varepsilon
are constants, then the problem is in
\mathrm{NP}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

For each
3
-SAT instance
\phi
, there is a nonlocal game
G(\phi)
such that
its noisy game value is
1
if
\phi
is satisfiable, and below some constant
c
if

\phi
is not satisfiable.

5.3
and
5.4
are proved in
Sections

5.1
and
5.2
respectively.

5.1
The nondeterministic algorithm

We first present an upper bound on the number of noisy MES sufficient to approximate the value of a nonlocal game to an arbitrary precision.
The upper bound from
[
QY21
]
is
D=\exp(\operatorname{poly}(s)
,
\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}(t)\right))
.
The follow-up work
[
QY23
]
studied fully quantum games in which both questions and answers are quantum and proved a better upper bound
D=\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}(s),\operatorname{poly}(t)\right)
using a refined Gaussian dimension reduction. We observe that this upper bound can be further improved to
D=\operatorname{poly}\left(s,\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}(t)\right)\right)
for nonlocal games.

Theorem 5.5
.

Given parameters
0<\epsilon,\rho<1
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
m\geq 2
, a noisy MES state
\psi_{AB}
, i.e.,
\psi_{A}=\psi_{B}=\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}
with the quantum maximal correlation
\rho=\rho\left(\psi_{AB}\right)<1

as defined in
Definition

2.1
, let
\mathfrak{G}
be a nonlocal game with the question sets
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
and the answer sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
. Suppose the players share arbitrarily many copies of
\psi_{AB}
. Let
\omega_{n}(\mathfrak{G},\psi_{AB})
be the highest winning probability that the players can achieve when sharing
n
copies of
\psi_{AB}
. Then there exists an explicitly computable bound
D=D\left(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{B}\rvert,m,\epsilon,\rho\right)
, such that
for any
n>D
,
\omega_{n}(\mathfrak{G},\psi_{AB})-\omega_{D}(\mathfrak{G},\psi_{AB})\leq\epsilon.

In particular, one may choose

D=\mathrm{poly}\left(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert,\exp\left(\mathrm{poly}\left(\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{B}\rvert,\frac{1}{\epsilon},\frac{1}{1-\rho}\right),\log m\right)\right).

The proof largely follows the framework in
[
QY21
]
with several refinements. We include it in
Appendix

C
.
7
7

7

One may wonder why the upper bound in
[
QY23
]
is still exponential in the size of the question set with the refined Gaussian dimension reduction. This is because of the different treatment of the questions. When the questions are classical, we take into account the distribution of the questions. However, if the questions are quantum as considered in
[
QY23
]
, the question registers are expressed as a linear combination of matrix basis elements, where an extra factor on the size of the question sets is introduced.
.

Next we present the algorithm, shown in
Fig.

2
, which is deterministic provided with a certificate.
By
Theorem

5.5
we know that sharing
{D}
copies of
\psi_{AB}
is sufficient to approximate the game value. However, outlining a strategy that shares
{D}
copies of
\psi_{AB}
requires
\exp\left({D}\right)
bits, rendering it excessively costly. Despite this, we’ve devised a more affordable certificate. Interpreted as a degree-
{d}
pseudo-strategy, this certificate is presented through its Fourier coefficients. By pseudo-strategy we mean two sets of operators
\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\}
and
\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}
that may not be a valid quantum strategy. However, we can still define the winning probability on a
pseudo-strategy, mathematically.

Definition 5.6
.

We summarize the parameters we use for the algorithm in the table below.

•

C_{pt}=300
.

•

\varepsilon_{rd}=\varepsilon^{2}/(4t^{3})
.

•

\delta=\frac{\varepsilon_{rd}^{2}}{C_{pt}t(t+1)}
.

•

{d}=\frac{C_{sm}\log^{2}\frac{1}{\delta}}{\delta(1-\rho)}
as in
Lemma

A.1
.

•

{s_{w}}=D\log m+\log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)
as in
Lemma

D.1
.

•

{D}
is the polynomial specified in
Theorem

5.5
with
\varepsilon\leftarrow\varepsilon/2
.

Input:

Parameters in
Definition

5.1
.

Certificate:

Let
\left\{\left(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
be a pair of standard orthonormal bases satisfying
2.6
.
A tuple of real numbers of width
s_{w}
, which are non-zero Fourier coefficients of
a degree-
{d}
pseudo-strategy on
{D}
copies of
\psi_{AB}
.
For each
x\in\mathcal{X},a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{D}
satisfying
\lvert\sigma\rvert\leq{d}
,
the certificate should contain the coefficient
\widehat{P}^{\,x}_{a}(\sigma)
.
Similarly, for
y\in\mathcal{Y},b\in\mathcal{B}
and
\sigma
, the certificate should contain the coefficient
\widehat{Q}^{\,y}_{b}(\sigma)
.
Then the degree-
{d}
pseudo-strategy can be written as
P^{\,x}_{a}
and
Q^{\,y}_{b}
satisfying

P^{\,x}_{a}=\sum_{\lvert\sigma\rvert\leq{d}}\widehat{P}^{\,x}_{a}(\sigma)\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}~\mbox{and}~Q^{\,y}_{b}=\sum_{\lvert\sigma\rvert\leq{d}}\widehat{Q}^{\,y}_{b}(\sigma)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}.

Algorithm:

Perform the following steps

1.

Compute the winning probability on the pseudo-strategy, which is

\mathrm{val}_{{D}}\left(\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)=\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y)\cdot V(x,y,a,b)\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{D}}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P}^{\,x}_{a}(\sigma)\cdot\widehat{Q}^{\,y}_{b}(\sigma),

where
c_{\sigma}=c_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots c_{\sigma_{D}}
, and
\left\{c_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is given in
2.6
.
Reject if

\mathrm{val}_{{D}}\left(\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)<\beta.

2.

Check if the operators sum up to the identity by checking

•

For all
x,y
and
\sigma\neq 0^{D}
, it should hold that

\sum_{a}\widehat{P}^{\,x}_{a}(\sigma)=\sum_{b}\widehat{Q}^{\,y}_{b}(\sigma)=0.

•

For all
x,y
, and
\sigma=0^{D}
, it should hold that

\sum_{a}\widehat{P}^{\,x}_{a}(\sigma)=\sum_{b}\widehat{Q}^{\,y}_{b}(\sigma)=1.

Reject if any of the above equalities fails.

3.

For each
x,y,a,b
,
run the positivity testing algorithm described in
Section

4
on
P^{\,x}_{a}
and
Q^{\,y}_{b}
with parameters
\beta\leftarrow 4\delta
and
\delta\leftarrow 2\delta
.
Reject if any of the positivity tests fails.

4.

Accept.

Figure 2:
Nondeterministic algorithm solving the noisy nonlocal game value problem

Time complexity
.
We upper bound the time complexity of each step.

1.

Certificate length: The certificate contains the non-zero Fourier coefficients of degree-
{d}
operators acting on
{D}
qudits.
Each degree-
{d}
operator consists of

\sum_{d=0}^{{d}}{\binom{{D}}{d}}\cdot(m^{2}-1)^{d}\leq{d}(m^{2}-1)^{d}{D}^{d}

coefficients, each
{s_{w}}
bits. Hence, the length of the certificate is
O(st{d}m^{2{d}}{D}^{d}{s_{w}})
.

2.

To compute the game value, we need to enumerate over all
x,y,a,b,\sigma
and compute a sum of products.
This takes time

s^{2}t^{2}(m^{2}-1)^{d}D^{d}.

3.

Check if the operators sum up to the identity takes linear time in certificate length as it involves only summation over Fourier coefficients.

4.

Each positivity test takes time as specified in
Theorem

4.2
, which is

\exp\left(\operatorname{poly}\left(m^{d},1/\delta\right)\right)\cdot D^{O(d)}.

By the choices of parameters in
Definition

5.6
, the overall running time is upper bounded by

\operatorname{poly}\left(s,\operatorname{eexp}\left(t,\frac{1}{1-\rho},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

Completeness
.
Suppose
\omega^{*}(G,\psi_{AB})\geq\beta+\varepsilon
.
Then by
Theorem

5.5
,
there exists a strategy
\left(P^{\,x}_{a},Q^{\,y}_{b}\right)
that uses
{D}
copies of
\psi_{AB}
with game value
\mathrm{val}_{{D}}\left(\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)\geq\beta+\varepsilon/2
.
Let
f
be the smoothing map in
Lemma

A.1
, and
let
{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}=f(P^{\,x}_{a})
and
{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}=f(Q^{\,y}_{b})
.
Then
\left\{{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right\}
are of degree at most
{d}
and satisfy

1.

For all
x,y
, we have
\sum_{a}={P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}=\sum_{b}{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}=\mathds{1}
(since
f
is linear and unital)

2.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1~\mbox{and}~{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1
.

3.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\otimes{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{\,x}_{a}\otimes Q^{\,y}_{b}\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)\right|\leq\delta
.

4.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right)\leq\delta
and
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right)\leq\delta
.

We observe that
Lemma

A.1
also guarantees
the Fourier coefficients of
{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}
and
{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}
have absolute values
bounded by
1
.
This allows us to truncate the strategy.
For each Fourier coefficient we preserve
{s_{w}}
digits and by
Lemma

D.1
get
\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}
satisfying

1.

For all
x,y
,
\sum_{a}{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}=\sum_{b}{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}=\mathds{1}
.

2.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1~\mbox{and}~{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1;

3.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left({P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\otimes{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\otimes{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)\right|\leq\delta,

4.

For all
x,y,a,b
,
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right)\leq 2\delta
and
m^{-{D}}\,\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right)\leq 2\delta.

This pseudo-strategy is the certificate.
Specifically, by
Lemma

A.6
the game value is

\mathrm{val}_{{D}}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}\right)\geq\beta+\varepsilon/2-2\delta t^{2}=\beta+\varepsilon/2-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2tC_{pt}}\geq\beta,

and the first check is passed.
Also, by item 4, the positivity tests can also be passed.

Soundness
.
Suppose that there exists a certificate that passes all tests,
then there exists a degree-
d
pseudo-strategy
\left\{{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right\}
satisfying

•

By the game value testing,

\mathrm{val}_{{D}}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right\}\right)\geq\beta.

•

By “summing up to the identity” testings, for all
x,y

\sum_{a}{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}=\mathds{1},~\mbox{and}~\sum_{b}{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}=\mathds{1}.

•

By the positivity tests,
for all
x,y,a,b

\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right)\leq 6\delta,~\mbox{and}~\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right)\leq 6\delta.

We then apply
Lemma

A.4
to get a strategy

\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\}
and
\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}
.
It holds that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}-{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\leq 3(t+1)\left(\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right)\right)+6\sqrt{t}\left(\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left({P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right)\right)^{1/2}\\
\leq 18t(t+1)\delta+6\sqrt{6}t\sqrt{\delta}\leq\frac{18\varepsilon_{rd}^{2}}{C_{pt}}+\frac{6\sqrt{6}\varepsilon_{rd}}{\sqrt{C_{pt}}}\leq\frac{18+6\sqrt{6C_{pt}}}{C_{pt}}\varepsilon_{rd}\leq\varepsilon_{rd}.

Similarly, for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
we have

\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}-{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{rd}.

We get a strategy
\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\}
and
\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}
with game value

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{val}_{D}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}\right)-\mathrm{val}_{D}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right\}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{val}_{D}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}-{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}\right)\right|+\left|\mathrm{val}_{D}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}-{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right\}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y)\left({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}-{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}-{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{b}\sum_{x,a}\mu_{A}(x){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}-{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{a}\sum_{y,b}\mu_{B}(y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{b}\sum_{x,a}\mu_{A}(x){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{P^{\,x,(1)}_{a}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{a}\sum_{y,b}\mu_{B}(y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}-{Q^{\,y,(1)}_{b}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\quad\mbox{(Cauchy-Schwartz)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2t\sqrt{t\varepsilon_{rd}}.

Thus there exists a strategy with game value

\mathrm{val}_{D}\left(\left\{{P^{\,x,(2)}_{a}}\right\},\left\{{Q^{\,y,(2)}_{b}}\right\}\right)>\beta-2t\sqrt{t\varepsilon_{rd}}=\beta-\varepsilon.

5.2
NP-hardness

In this subsection, we first show that if
L\in\mathrm{MIP}[s,t]
with perfect completeness and constant soundness, then
L\in\mathrm{MIP}^{*}[s,t,\psi_{AB}]
also with perfect completeness and constant soundness.

Setup:

Flip two unbiased coins
\boldsymbol{c}_{1},\boldsymbol{c}_{2}\sim\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Sample questions
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\sim\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}(\mathsf{input})
.
With probability
1/2
each, perform one of the following two tests.

Verify:

Distribute the questions as follows

•

Player
\boldsymbol{c}_{1}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}
: give
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
\boldsymbol{b}

Accept if
V(\mathsf{input},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
accepts on
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}
.

Consistency:

Distribute the questions as follows:
if
\boldsymbol{c}_{2}=0

•

Player
\boldsymbol{c}_{1}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}
,

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}
; receive
\boldsymbol{b}
,

otherwise

•

Player
\boldsymbol{c}_{1}
: give
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}
,

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}
: give
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
\boldsymbol{b}
,

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{b}
.

Figure 3:
The noisy
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
verifier
V^{*}
from an
\mathrm{MIP}
verifier
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}
protocol for a language
L
with perfect completeness.
Then the verifier
V^{*}
described in
Fig.

3
is an
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
verifier for
L

with the following conditions:

Completeness.

If
\mathsf{input}\in L
, there is a value-
1
strategy for
V^{*}
.

Soundsness.

Given
\mathsf{input}
, if there is a strategy for
V^{*}
with value
1-\epsilon
where the provers share
arbitrarily many copies of a noisy MES, then there is
a classical strategy for
V
with value
1-2\varepsilon-\frac{32\epsilon}{1-\rho}
.

Proof of
5.4
.

Notice that
3
-SAT
\in\mathrm{MIP}[\log,1]

[
BFL91
]
with perfect completeness and an arbitrarily small constant soundness. By
Theorem

5.7
, there exists an
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}[\log,1,\psi_{AB}]
protocol for
3
-SAT with perfect completeness and a constant soundness.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.7
.

Completeness.
If
\mathsf{input}
is satisfiable, the value-
1
strategy for
V
is also a value-
1
strategy for
V^{*}
.

Soundness.
In the consistency test, with probability
1/2
both provers get Alice’s question
x
. Suppose that Alice and Bob share
n
copies of a noisy
m
-dimensional MES
\psi_{AB}
, and that they apply the measurements
\left\{P^{x}_{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
and
\left\{Q^{x}_{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
, respectively. Hence the probability for the provers to pass the consistency test of
x
is at least
1-4\epsilon
.
It means that

\displaystyle\bigE_{x}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{x}_{a}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)\geq 1-4\epsilon.

Let
\left\{\left(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
be a pair of standard orthonormal bases satisfying
2.6
. Using the Fourier expansions of
P^{x}_{a}=\sum_{\sigma}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
and
Q^{x}_{a}=\sum_{\sigma}\widehat{Q^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}
, the condition above is equivalent to

\displaystyle\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)\widehat{Q^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)\geq 1-4\epsilon,

where
c_{\sigma}=c_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots c_{\sigma_{D}}
, and
\left\{c_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is given in
2.6
. By the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,

\displaystyle\left(\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{Q^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\geq 1-4\epsilon.

Notice that

\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{Q^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}\widehat{Q^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}=\sum_{a}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}\leq 1

for all
x
, we have

\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\geq(1-4\epsilon)^{2}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\left[\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2}+\rho\sum_{\sigma\neq 0^{n}}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(\sigma\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\left[\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2}+\rho({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}-\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\rho+(1-\rho)\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigE_{x}\sum_{a}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2}\geq 1-\frac{8\epsilon-16\epsilon^{2}}{1-\rho}\geq 1-\frac{8\epsilon}{1-\rho}.

(23)

Note that for all
x
,
\sum_{a}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)=m^{-n}\sum_{a}\mathrm{Tr}~P^{x}_{a}=1
.
For each
x
, let
a_{x}
be the answer that maximizes
\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)
.
Then
\sum_{a}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)^{2}\leq\widehat{P^{x}_{a_{x}}}\left(0^{n}\right)\sum_{a}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}\left(0^{n}\right)=\widehat{P^{x}_{a_{x}}}\left(0^{n}\right)
, and

\displaystyle\bigE_{x}\widehat{P^{x}_{a_{x}}}\left(0^{n}\right)\geq 1-\frac{8\epsilon}{1-\rho}.

Similarly, from the fact that they can pass the consistency test on Bob’s questions, we can conclude that the measurements
\left\{Q^{y}_{b}\right\}
satisfy the conditions above.
In particular, let
b_{y}
be the answer that maximizes
\widehat{Q^{y}_{b}}\left(0^{n}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle\bigE_{y}\widehat{Q^{y}_{b_{y}}}\left(0^{n}\right)\geq 1-\frac{8\epsilon}{1-\rho}.

In the deterministic strategy, Alice answers
a_{x}
for question
x
and Bob answers
b_{y}
for question
y
.
The difference in the probability of satisfying
V
between the original strategy and the deterministic strategy is

\displaystyle\left|\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{a,b}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]V(x,y,a,b)-\bigE_{x,y}V(x,y,a_{x},b_{y})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\bigE_{x,y}\left(1-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a_{x}}\otimes Q^{y}_{b_{y}}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]\right)V(x,y,a_{x},b_{y})+\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\neq a_{x}\text{ or}\\
b\neq b_{y}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]V(x,y,a,b)

\displaystyle\leq\bigE_{x,y}\left(1-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a_{x}}\otimes Q^{y}_{b_{y}}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]\right)+\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\neq a_{x}\text{ or}\\
b\neq b_{y}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]

where we use the fact that
V(x,y,a,b)\leq 1
for all
x,y,a,b
.
Writing
1=\sum_{a,b}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]
, we get that the expression above equals

\displaystyle 2\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\neq a_{x}\text{ or}\\
b\neq b_{y}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{a\neq a_{x},b}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]+2\bigE_{x,y}\sum_{b\neq b_{y}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(P^{x}_{a_{x}}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\bigE_{x,y}\left[\sum_{a\neq a_{x}}\widehat{P^{x}_{a}}(0^{n})+\sum_{b\neq b_{y}}\widehat{Q^{y}_{b}}(0^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{32\epsilon}{1-\rho}.

The probability for the original strategy to satisfy
V
is at least
1-2\varepsilon
,
so the probability for the deterministic strategy to satisfy
V
is at least
1-2\varepsilon-32\varepsilon/(1-\rho)
.
∎

6
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
protocol for
\mathrm{RE}
with
O(1)
-size answers

In this section, we prove that there is an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
protocol for any language in
\mathrm{RE}
with poly-size questions and constant-size answers.
The first step is to tighten the answer reduction techniques from
[
JNV+20a
,
NZ23
]
so that they can reduce the verifier’s answer size sequetially from
\operatorname{poly}(n)
to
\operatorname{polylog}(n)
, and then to
\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)
.
The second step is to develop a new answer reduction technique that can reduce the answer size of an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
protocol from
\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)
to
O(1)
while preserving other parameters of the protocol.

We achieve the second step by modifying the answer reduction technique from
[
NW19
]
.
Natarajan and Wright’s answer reduction follows a modular design with two major components: Probabilistically checkable proofs of proximity (
\mathrm{PCPP}
) and a tester of the low-degree code.
Hence, to achieve constant answer size, it suffices to change the code to be the Hadamard code, and derive a new tester for the Hadamard code
that allows a verifier to test multiple bits of a codeword at the same time.
Then in our final construction of the
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}[\operatorname{poly},O(1)]
protocol for
\mathrm{RE}
, we successively apply the tightened answer reduction technique, followed by the new technique with the Hadamard code, to the
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}[\operatorname{poly},\operatorname{poly}]
protocol for
\mathrm{RE}

[
JNV+20a
]
.

Note that
[
JNV+20a
]
doesn’t use the answer reduction technique of
[
NW19
]
.
The authors of
[
JNV+20a
]
use a specific
\mathrm{PCPP}
tailored to the low individual-degree code in their answer reduction technique so that it fits the recursive compression framework.
However, the answer reduction technique of
[
JNV+20a
]
is more difficult to modify due to its less modular design.

6.1
Tighter answer reduction

For
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
protocols with short answers, it is useful to separate the part of the verifier that directly acts on the answer bits from the rest. Without loss of generality, we imagine that the decider
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
acts in two phases: first, given its internal randomness and the question pair
x,y
, it computes a Boolean circuit
C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x,y}
, and then it applies
C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x,y}
to the answers and returns its output. The
verification time
is the total runtime of this process. We define the
decision complexity
denoted by
d_{V,A}(n)
to be the maximal size of the circuit
C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x,y}
over all
\mathsf{input}
of size
n
and question
x,y
sampled by
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}(\mathsf{input})
.
This is always at most as large as the verification time, but it can be much smaller.

In this section, we will observe that tighter bounds on the answer reduction theorem of
[
JNV+20a
,
NZ23
]
can be given in terms of the decider complexity.
In particular, the next theorem is an improved version of
[
NZ23
, Theorem 51]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
protocol for a language
L
, with question length
\ell_{V,Q}(n)
,
answer length
\ell_{V,A}(n)
,
sampling time
t_{V,Q}(n)
, verification time
t_{V,A}(n)
, and decision complexity
d_{V,A}(n)
.
Suppose further that
V
has the following property: for any
\mathsf{input}\in L
, the prover has a
real commuting symmetric EPR strategy of value
1
.
Then there is an answer-reduced verifier
V^{AR}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime},\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime})
with the following properties:

Question length.

The new question length is
2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(\log d_{V,A}(n))
.

Answer length.

The new answer length is
\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Sampling time.

The new sampling time is
t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Verification time.

The new verification time is
t_{V,Q}(n)+t_{V,A}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Decision complexity.

The new decision complexity is
