\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Completeness.

If
\mathsf{input}\in L
, there is a value-
1
real commuting symmetric EPR strategy for
V^{AR}
.

Soundness.

Given
\mathsf{input}
, if there is a strategy to
V^{AR}
with value
1-\epsilon
, then there is a strategy to
V
with value
1-\delta(\epsilon,n)
, where
\delta(\epsilon,n)=a((\log d_{V,A}(n))^{a}\epsilon^{b}+(\log d_{V,A}(n))^{-100b})
,
a
is a universal constant such that
a>0
, and
b
is a universal constant such that
0<b<1
.

Efficient computability.

There exists an algorithm that takes the description of
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
as input and outputs the description of
V^{AR}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime},\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime})
in time
O(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)
,
where
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert
and
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert
denote the sizes of the descriptions of
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
and
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
respectively.
Moreover,
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+O(1)
and
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert+O(1)
.

Proof.

The proof will follow
[
NZ23
]
very closely. The main nontrivial thing to prove is the bound on the decision complexity of
V^{AR}
.

In more detail, the answer-reduced verifier first apply the Cook-Levin theorem to the circuit
C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x,y}
computed in the first phase of
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
to
get a 5SAT instance with size
s=\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n))
.
Following the proof of
[
NZ23
, Theorem 51]
, we can choose the parameters used in the PCP part of the answer reduction techniques as

\displaystyle m=O(\log s)=O(\log(d_{V,A}(n))),

\displaystyle m^{\prime}=5m+5=O(\log(d_{V,A}(n))),

\displaystyle q=2^{O(n)}=\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n)),

\displaystyle d=O(m)=O(\log(d_{V,A}(n))),

so that the proofs of the provers are
m^{\prime}+6
low-degree polynomials on
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
and
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m^{\prime}}
of individual degree at most
d
in each variable.

Question size.
The question of
V^{AR}
has at most two questions sampled by
V
and queries to the low-degree polynomials prepared by the provers. Hence,

\displaystyle\ell_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+O(m^{\prime}\log q)=2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)).

Answer size.
The answer size is at most
O((m^{\prime}+6)(d+1)\log q)
, which is the number of bits to specify the coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of
m^{\prime}+6
polynomials of degree at most
d
. Hence,

\displaystyle\ell_{V^{AR},A}(n)=O(m^{\prime}d\log q)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)).

Sampling time.
The verifier will first sample the questions
x,y
and then sample queries to the low-degree polynomials. Following the proof of
[
JNV+20a
, Theorem 10.27]
, we have

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(m^{\prime},\log q)=t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)).

Verification time.
Following the description of the answer-reduced verifier in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 14]
,
we can see that the run time of the answer-reduced verifier is sum of the run time of each step.
Step 1 and 2 are consistency checks, so the run time is
O(\ell_{V^{AR},A}(n))
.
Step 3 and 4 are low-degree tests, so the run time is at most
\operatorname{poly}(m,d,m^{\prime},\log q)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.
In Step 5,
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime}
needs to run the functions
L^{A}
and
L^{B}
of the original
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
first, which takes time
t_{V,Q}(n)
and then
the PCP verification.
The PCP verification takes time
\operatorname{poly}(s)+\operatorname{poly}(s,\log q)+\operatorname{poly}(m^{\prime},\log q)+\operatorname{poly}(\log q)=\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n))
according to the proof of
[
JNV+20a
, Theorem 10.25]
. Hence, overall,

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},A}(n)=t_{V,Q}(n)+t_{V,A}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n)).

Decision complexity.
The checks performed by
V^{AR}
in
[
NZ23
]
are the same as those in
[
JNV+20a
]
,
which are specified in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 14]
.
These checks are simple arithmetics over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
as explained below.

1.

Step 1 and 2 of
V^{AR}
as in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 14]
are consistency checks.

2.

Step 3 and 4 of
V^{AR}
as in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 14]
are low-degree test, which is evaluating a univariate polynomial over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of degree at most
m^{\prime}d
, whose coefficients are specified by the prover, at a point chosen by the verifier.
Note that the checks in these 4 steps do not depend on
C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x,y}
.

3.

In Step 5, the checks of the PCP verifier are executed:

(a)

In Step 4 of the PCP verifier as in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 13]
, the values of
\mathcal{F}_{arith}(x,o,w),o_{1},\dots,o_{5}
are precomputed, so the verifier only needs to evaluate an individual-degree-1 polynomial on the prover’s answers.

(b)

In Step 5 of the PCP veifier as in
[
JNV+20a
, Figure 13]
, the values of
\mathrm{zero}(z_{i})
are precomputed, so again the verifier only needs to evaluate an individual-degree-1 polynomial on the prover’s answers.

Hence,
d_{V^{AR},A}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(\ell_{V^{AR},A}(n))=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Completeness, soundness and efficient computability
follow from the same argument in the proof of
[
NZ23
, Theorem 51]
.
The soundness error is calculated using the values of
q,m,m^{\prime}
and
d
in our setting.
∎

We will actually use a parallel-repeated version of this answer reduction, which obtains constant soundness. This is closely modeled on
[
NZ23
, Theorem 52]
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
protocol for a language
L
, with question length
\ell_{V,Q}
, sampling time
t_{V,Q}
, verification time
t_{V,A}
, and decision complexity
d_{V,A}
.
Suppose further that
V
has the following property: for any
\mathsf{input}\in L
, the prover has a
real commuting symmetric EPR strategy with a value
1
.
Then there exists an efficiently computable function
k(n)=\operatorname{poly}(\log d_{V,A}(n))
and an answer-reduced verifier
V^{AR}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime},\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime})

such that the following hold:

Question length.

The new question length is
k(n)\cdot(2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(\log d_{V,A}(n)))
.

Answer length.

The new answer length is
k(n)\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Sampling time.

The new sampling time is
k(n)\cdot(t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)))
.

Verification time.

The new verification time is
k(n)\cdot(t_{V,Q}(n)+t_{V,A}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n)))
.

Decision complexity.

The new decision complexity is
k(n)\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

Completeness.

If
\mathsf{input}\in L
, there is a value-
1
strategy for
V^{AR}
.

Soundness.

Given
\mathsf{input}
, if the value of
\,V
is at most
1/2
, then the value of
\,V^{AR}
on
\mathsf{input}
is also at most
1/2
.

Efficient computability.

There exists an algorithm that takes the description of
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
as input and outputs the description of
V^{AR}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime},\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime})
in time
O(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)
.
Moreover,
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+O(1)
and
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert+O(1)
.

The choice of
k(n)
follows the same reasoning in the proof of
[
NZ23
, Theorem 52]
.
The only difference is that here
s
, the size of the SAT formular, is
\operatorname{poly}(d_{V,A}(n))
, instead of
\operatorname{poly}(t_{V,A}(n),\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)
, so
k(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(s)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))
.

We note that, unlike in the answer reduction theorems of
[
JNV+20a
]
, we do not keep track of the
level
of the sampler in our answer reduction theorems. To recall, the level is a measure of the complexity of the sampler distribution when expressed in terms of a “conditional linear” function of a uniform random seed. The level is important in the context of
[
JNV+20a
]
because it affects the complexity of the question reduction procedure, in which the sampling of questions is delegated to the provers. Thus, in that paper, it was important to keep track of the level to make sure that it remains bounded by a universal constant, so that question reduction can be recursively applied. In our setting, we will never apply question reduction directly, so we do not need to track the level. It can be checked that the answer reduction theorems as stated here hold for all levels, and all of the asymptotic bounds are independent of the number of levels.

Remark 6.3
.

The important conclusion from
Theorem

6.2
is that answer reduction shrinks the decision complexity of a protocol. Looking ahead, this will help us when we repeatedly apply answer reduction. One might ask how the decision complexity behaves under
question reduction
, which is the other component of the compression procedure in
[
JNV+20a
]
. It turns out that question reduction will in general always “reset” the decision complexity to be the same as the decider runtime prior to question reduction. This is because question reduction delegates sampling the questions to the provers, so the “new” decider, after question reduction has been applied, must wait for the “new” answers in order to simulate the computation of the old decider. Thus, even if the old decider could do most of its work as precomputation before the answers were received, the new decider may not be able to do any precomputation, so the decision complexity can only be bounded by the runtime of the old decider.

6.2
Subset tester for the Hadamard code

To use the
[
NW19
]
answer reduction procedure with a particular error-correcting code, one must show that this code satisfies certain efficient testability properties. Here we show this for the Hadamard code. Specifically, we show that the Hadamard code has a
subset tester
in the sense of
[
NW19
, Section 16]
, shown in
Fig.

4
, which ensures that the provers have a global Hadamard encoding of some bitstring.

First, we recall the definition and key properties of the Hadamard code.

Definition 6.4
.

The Hadamard code encodes
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
as
\mathsf{Enc}_{k}(x)=(x\cdot y)_{y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}
. Moreover,

•

For
x\neq y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,
\mathsf{Enc}_{k}(x)
and
\mathsf{Enc}_{k}(y)
have normalized Hamming agreement at most
\eta_{H}=\frac{1}{2}
.

•

There exists an embedding
\mu_{k}:[k]\to[2^{k}]
such that for each
i\in[k]
,
\mu_{k}(i)=2^{i-1}
and
x_{i}=(\mathsf{Enc}(x))_{\mu_{k}(i)}
.

•

There exists a decoding algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}_{k}
such that
\mathsf{Dec}_{k}(\mathsf{Enc}_{k}(x))=x
and, for every
w
not in the range of
\mathsf{Enc}_{k}
,
\mathsf{Dec}_{k}(w)=\perp
.

The decoding algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}_{k}
on input
w
, first computes
x=(w_{\mu_{k}(k)},\ldots,w_{\mu_{k}(1)})

outputs
x
if
w=\mathsf{Enc}_{k}(x)
and
\perp
otherwise.
Hence the running time of the decoding algorithm is
t_{\mathsf{Dec}}(k)=O(2^{k}).

Note that both
\mathsf{Enc}_{k}
and
\mathsf{Dec}_{k}
run in time exponential in
k
.

Let
k\leq n
and
D
be a distribution on the subsets of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
with size
k
.
Flip an unbiased coin
\boldsymbol{b}\sim\left\{0,1\right\}
.
Sample
\boldsymbol{F}=\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\right\}\sim D
and a uniformly random
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
,
Perform one of the following three subtests with equal probability.

Subtest 1:

Perform one of the following checks with equal probability.

Check 1:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k+1}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
, receive
(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{d}_{k})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{a}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
for all
i
.

Check 2:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k+1}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{y}
, receive
\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{a}_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
, and
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{c}
.

Check 3:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k+1}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{y}=\left\{\boldsymbol{x}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}+\boldsymbol{y}\right\}
, receive
(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{d}_{k})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{a}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{i}=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
for all
i
.

Subtest 2:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{y}=\left\{\boldsymbol{x}_{1}+\boldsymbol{y},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}+\boldsymbol{y}\right\}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k})
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}+\boldsymbol{y}
for a random
i
, receive
\boldsymbol{d}
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}=\boldsymbol{d}
.

Subtest 3:

Perform one of the following checks with equal probability

Check 1:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{k})
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{F}
; receive
(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{d}_{k})
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
for all
i
.

Check 2:

Distribute the question as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}+\boldsymbol{y}
for a random
i
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}+\boldsymbol{y}
for a random
i
; receive
\boldsymbol{d}
.

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{d}
.

Figure 4:
Subset tester for the Hadamard code

Proposition 6.5
.

For the subset
F=\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
sampled according to a distribution
D
and a uniformly random
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
,
if a quantum strategy with
\left|\psi\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
and measurements

\displaystyle\left\{M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\mid a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k},c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\right\},\left\{N^{F}_{b}\mid b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\right\},\left\{N^{y}_{d}\mid d\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\right\}

can pass the subset tester with probability
1-\varepsilon
, then there is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{B}
,
a state
\left|aux\right\rangle=\left|aux_{A}\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{B}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{B}
and a projective measurement
\left\{\hat{G}_{u}\mid u\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\right\}

on
\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}^{\prime}

such that if we write
\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle=\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux\right\rangle

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\lVert N^{F}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:u\cdot x_{i}=a_{i}\\
\forall i\in[k]\end{subarray}}\hat{G}_{u}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq(2k-1)^{2}(45+12\sqrt{k})\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Let
F+y=(x_{1}+y,\ldots,x_{k}+y)
.
Let

\displaystyle\Omega=\left\{(a,c,a^{\prime})\mid a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for all }i\in[k]\right\}.

The set
\Omega
is the set of valid answer tuples for Alice in
Subtest 1
; we also use
\Omega
to denote the
event
that Alice’s answers are valid.
Winning the subset tester with probability
1-\varepsilon
implies that
winning each subtest with a probability of at least
1-3\varepsilon
.
Furthermore,
winning
Subtest 1
with a probability of at least
1-3\varepsilon
implies that
when Alice gets question
(F,y)
and Bob gets Player
1
’s questions:

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[a_{1}=b_{1}\land\ldots\land a_{k}=b_{k}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[c=d\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=y]\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[a^{\prime}_{1}=b_{1}\land\ldots\land a_{k}^{\prime}=b_{k}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F+y]\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y)]\geq 1-6\varepsilon,

for all
i\in[k]
;
winning
Subtest 2
with a probability of at least
1-3\varepsilon
implies that
when Alice gets Player 0’s question and Bob gets Player 1’s question

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[a_{i}=d\mid q_{A}=F+y,q_{B}=x_{i}+y]\geq 1-6k\varepsilon;

and winning
Subtest 3
with a probability of at least
1-3\varepsilon
implies that
when Alice gets Player 0’s question and Bob gets Player 1’s question

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\Pr[a_{1}=b_{1}\land\ldots\land a_{k}=b_{k}\mid q_{A}=q_{B}=F]\geq 1-12\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\sim D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[a=b\mid q_{A}=q_{B}=x_{i}+y]\geq 1-12k\varepsilon\quad\text{ for all }i.

In terms of the measurements and the state
\left|\psi\right\rangle
, these conditions are equivalent to

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,a^{\prime}:\\
a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\forall i\end{subarray}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\otimes N^{F}_{a}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,a^{\prime}:\\
a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\forall i\end{subarray}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\otimes N^{y}_{c}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,a^{\prime}:\\
a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\forall i\end{subarray}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\otimes N^{F+y}_{a^{\prime}}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-18\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,a^{\prime}:\\
a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\forall i\end{subarray}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-6\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{F+y}_{a}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y}_{a_{i}}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-6k\varepsilon\quad\text{ for all }i

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{F}_{a}\otimes N^{F}_{a}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-12\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}+y}_{a}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y}_{a}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 1-12k\varepsilon\quad\text{ for all }i.

We define binary observables

\displaystyle M^{x_{i}|F,y}=\sum_{a,c,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{a_{i}}M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle M^{y|F,y}=\sum_{a,c,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{c}M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}=\sum_{a,c,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{a_{i}^{\prime}}M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle N^{x_{i}|F}=\sum_{b}(-1)^{b_{i}}N^{F}_{b}

\displaystyle N^{y}=N^{y}_{0}-N^{y}_{1}

\displaystyle N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}=\sum_{b}(-1)^{b_{i}}N^{F+y}_{b}.

We can prove

\displaystyle\quad\quad\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{x_{i}|F,y}\otimes N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{x\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Big[\Pr[a_{i}=b_{i}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(\Pr[a_{i}\neq b_{i}\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]-\Pr[a_{i}=b_{i}\land\overline{\Omega}\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F])\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Big[\Pr[a_{i}=b_{i}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]-(1-\Pr[a_{i}=b_{i}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F])\Big]

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Big[2\Pr[a_{i}=b_{i}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]-1\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Big[2\Pr[a_{1}=b_{1}\land\ldots\land a_{k}=b_{k}\land\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y),q_{B}=F]-1\Big]

\displaystyle\geq 1-36\varepsilon,

which implies that
\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert M^{x_{i}|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 72\varepsilon
by expanding the vector norm.
Similarly, from the two other checks of
Subtest 1
,

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert M^{y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{y}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 72\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 72\varepsilon.

Applying a similar argument to the probability of the event
\Omega
, we can also show

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{x_{i}|F,y}M^{y|F,y}M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{a,c,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{a_{i}+c+a_{i}^{\prime}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{F,y}_{a,c,a^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}2\Pr[a_{i}+c=a_{i}^{\prime}\mid q_{A}=(F,y)]-1

\displaystyle\geq\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}2\Pr[\Omega\mid q_{A}=(F,y)]-1\geq 1-12\varepsilon.

Next, we would like to replace
M^{x_{i}|F,y}
by
N^{x_{i}|F}
,
M^{y|F,y}
by
N^{y}
and
M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}
by
N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}

and show

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}N^{y}N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle-1\rvert\leq 38\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

(24)

In the first step

\displaystyle\quad\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{x_{i}|F,y}M^{y|F,y}(M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert M^{y|F,y}M^{x_{i}|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert\cdot\lVert(M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert\quad\mbox{(Cauchy-Schwarz)}

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert(M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert(M^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}}\quad\mbox{(Jensen)}

\displaystyle\leq 6\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|M^{x_{i}|F,y}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F,y}\cdot(M^{y|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rvert\leq 6\sqrt{2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}N^{y}\cdot(M^{x_{i}|F,y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}|F})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rvert\leq 6\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

Hence

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}N^{y}N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle-1\rvert\leq 18\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+12\varepsilon\leq 38\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

On the other hand, from
Subtest 2
, we have that for all
i\in[k]

\displaystyle\quad\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\Pr[a_{i}=b\mid q_{A}=F+y,q_{B}=x_{i}+y]-1\geq 1-12k\varepsilon,

which implies that

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert(N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 24k\varepsilon.

From
Subtest 3
, with similar reasoning we know

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\lVert(N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}|F})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 48\varepsilon

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\lVert(N^{x_{i}+y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y})\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq 48k\varepsilon\quad\text{ for all }i.

Then

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y|F+y}N^{y}N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{24k\varepsilon}}\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}+y}\otimes N^{y}N^{x_{i}|F}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{48\varepsilon}}\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}+y}N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes N^{y}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{48k\varepsilon}}\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y}N^{y}\left|\psi\right\rangle

Hence
Eq.

24
implies that

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes N^{x_{i}+y}N^{y}\left|\psi\right\rangle-1\rvert\leq(45+12\sqrt{k})\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

(25)

Let
C_{1}:=45+12\sqrt{k}
.
Let
\tilde{N}_{u}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}(-1)^{u\cdot y}N^{y}
and
G_{u}=(\tilde{N}_{u})^{2}
. Since each
N^{y}
is a binary observable,
\left\{G_{u}\right\}
is a POVM.
It can be checked that
N^{y}=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}(-1)^{u\cdot y}\tilde{N}_{u}
.
Averaging over
F\sim D
, the consistency between
\left\{N^{x_{i}|F}_{0},N^{x_{i}|F}_{1}\right\}
and
\left\{\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=0}G_{u},\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=1}G_{u}\right\}
,
where
N^{x_{i}|F}_{c}=\sum_{b:b_{i}=c}N^{F}_{b}
for
c=0,1
,
is

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\frac{1}{2}(1+\left\langle\psi\right|\sum_{u}(-1)^{u\cdot x_{i}}N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes G_{u}\left|\psi\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\psi\right|\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{y,z\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}\sum_{u}(-1)^{u\cdot(x_{i}+y+z)}N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes N^{y}N^{z}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\psi\right|\bigE_{F\sim D}\bigE_{z\in D_{\mathsf{Unif}}}N^{x_{i}|F}\otimes N^{x_{i}+z}N^{z}\left|\psi\right\rangle\approx_{\frac{C_{1}}{2}\sqrt{\varepsilon}}1,

which follows
Eq.

25
.
We consider the Naimark’s dialation of
\left\{G_{u}\right\}
on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
denoted by
\left\{\hat{G}_{u}\right\}
, which is a projective measurement.
There exists
\left|aux\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
such that
averaging over
F\sim D
, the consistency between
\left\{N^{x_{i}|F}_{0}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}},N^{x_{i}|F}_{1}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\right\}
and
\left\{\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=0}\hat{G}_{u},\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=1}\hat{G}_{u}\right\}
with respect to
\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle=\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux\right\rangle
is

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a=0,1}\left\langle\psi^{\prime}\right|(N^{x_{i}|F}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}})\otimes(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=a}\hat{G}_{u})\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a=0,1}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}|F}_{a}\otimes\left(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=a}(\mathds{1}\otimes\left\langle aux\right|)\hat{G}_{u}(\mathds{1}\otimes\left|aux\right\rangle)\right)\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a=0,1}\left\langle\psi\right|N^{x_{i}|F}_{a}\otimes\left(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=a}G_{u}\right)\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\approx_{C_{1}/2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}1.

Since both
\left\{N^{x_{i}|F}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\right\}
and
\left\{\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=a}\hat{G}_{u}\right\}
are projective measurements, their consistency implies that

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{d=0,1}\lVert N^{x_{i}|F}_{d}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle-\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{i}=d}\hat{G}_{u}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Next, notice that

\displaystyle N^{F}_{a}=N^{x_{k}|F}_{a_{k}}\ldots N^{x_{1}|F}_{a_{1}}\text{ and }\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:u\cdot x_{i}=a_{i}\\
\forall i\in[k]\end{subarray}}\hat{G}_{u}=\left(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{k}=a_{k}}\hat{G}_{u}\right)\ldots\left(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{1}=a_{1}}\hat{G}_{u}\right)\ldots\left(\sum_{u:u\cdot x_{k}=a_{k}}\hat{G}_{u}\right)

Then by
Lemma

2.36

\displaystyle\bigE_{F\sim D}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\lVert N^{F}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:u\cdot x_{i}=a_{i}\\
\forall i\in[k]\end{subarray}}\hat{G}_{u}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq(2k-1)^{2}C_{1}\sqrt{\varepsilon},

which completes the proof.
∎

6.3
Answer reduction protocol

The subset tester of the Hadamard code implies that we can replace the low-degree code of the answer reduction technique
in
[
NW19
, Section 17.4]
by the Hadamard code.
The other key ingredient of Natarajan and Wright’s answer reduction is Probabilistically Checkable Proofs of Proximity (PCPP),
so we recall its definition and key properties that we will use later.

Definition 6.6

(PCPP)
.

For functions
r,q:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
t:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
an
(r,q,t)
-restricted
\mathrm{PCPP}
verifier is a probabilistic machine that, given a string
x

(called the
explicit
input) and a number
K
(in binary) as well as oracle access to an implicit input
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{K}
and to a
proof oracle

\pi\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
,
tosses
r(\lvert x\rvert+K)
coins, queries the oracles
(y,\pi)
for a total of
q(\lvert x\rvert+K)
symbols,
runs in time
t(\lvert x\rvert,K)
, and outputs a Boolean verdict.

For constants
s,\gamma\in[0,1]
, a pair language
L\subseteq\left\{0,1\right\}^{\ast}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{\ast}
is in
\mathrm{PCPP}_{s,\gamma}[r,q,t]
if there exists an
(r,q,t)
-restricted
\mathrm{PCPP}

verifier
V
with the following properties:

Completeness:

If
(x,y)\in L
, there exists a proof
\pi
such that

\Pr_{R}[V^{y,\pi}(x,\lvert y\rvert;R)=1]=1
where
V^{y,\pi}(x,\lvert y\rvert;R)
denotes the decision of
V

on input
(x,\lvert y\rvert)
, oracle access to
(y,\pi)
, and randomness
R
.

Soundness:

Let
L_{x}=\left\{y\mid(x,y)\in L\right\}
. If
(x,y)
is such that
y
is
\gamma
-far from
L_{x}\cap\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lvert y\rvert}
, then for every
\pi
,
\Pr_{R}[V^{y,\pi}(x,\lvert y\rvert;R)=1]\leq s
.

We work with the
\mathrm{PCPP}
such that when
L
is an
\mathsf{NTIME}(T)
pair language,

Randomness complexity:

r(m)=\log_{2}T(m)+O(\log_{2}\log_{2}T(m))
,

Query complexity:

q(m)=O(1)
, and

Verification time:

t(n,K)=\operatorname{poly}(n,\log_{2}K,\log_{2}T(n+K))
.

We are going to apply the
\mathrm{PCPP}
defined above to the following language.

Definition 6.7
.

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
verifier, where
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
is his algorithm to sample the questions and
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
is his algorithm to check the answers.
Suppose on inputs of length
n
it has question length
\ell_{V,Q}(n)
and answer length
\ell_{V,A}(n)
.
We define

\displaystyle L_{\mathsf{Enc}}=\left\{(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{Enc}_{\ell_{V,A}(\lvert\mathsf{input}\rvert)}(y_{0}),\mathsf{Enc}_{\ell_{V,A}(\lvert\mathsf{input}\rvert)}(y_{1}))\mid C^{\mathsf{input}}_{x_{0},x_{1}}(y_{0},y_{1})=1\right\},

which are all the accepted tuples with the answers encoded by
\mathsf{Enc}_{\ell_{A}(\lvert\mathsf{input}\rvert)}
.

Note that when
\lvert\mathsf{input}\rvert=n
, the running time of the decider of
L_{\mathsf{Enc}}
is the maximal of the running time of
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
and
\mathsf{Dec}_{\ell_{V,A}(n)}
as pointed out in
[
NW19
, Proposition 17.7]
.
Suppose
\gamma\leq\eta_{H}/2=1/4
.
Then by
[
NW19
, Proposition 17.8]
, if
(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},z_{0},z_{1})
does not correspond to
the encoding of any assignment accepted by
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
, for every proof
\pi

\displaystyle\Pr_{R}[V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}^{z_{0},z_{1},\pi}(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},\lvert z_{0}\rvert+\lvert z_{1}\rvert;R)=1]\leq s

where
s
is the soundness of
V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}
.

Definition 6.8
.

We instantiate the answer-reduced
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
protocol with the following components and notations.

•

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
verifier for a Language
L
.
Suppose on inputs of size
n
, the verifier
V
has question length
\ell_{V,Q}(n)
,
answer length
\ell_{V,A}(n)
, question sampling time
t_{V,Q}(n)
and answer verification time
t_{V,A}(n)
.

•

Let
G_{k}(\boldsymbol{T})
be the subset tester from
Section

6.2
for the Hadamard code of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
with the embedding
\mu_{k}
, and for
the subset
\boldsymbol{T}
sampled according to some distribution
D
.

•

Let
L_{\mathsf{Enc}}
be the language defined in
Definition

6.7
, which is in
\text{TIME}(T)
with

\displaystyle T(n)=t_{V,A}(n)+t_{\mathsf{Dec}}(\ell_{V,A}(n)).

and let
V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}
be its
\mathrm{PCPP}
verifier with

\gamma\leq 1/4
and constant soundness
s
.
The verification time is

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{PCPP},A}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\log_{2}(2^{\ell_{V,A}(n)}),\log_{2}(T(n))).

The sampling time is also upper bounded by the verification time of the PCPP, which includes both the sampling time and answer verification time, so

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{PCPP},Q}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\log_{2}(2^{\ell_{V,A}(n)}),\log_{2}(T(n))).

Finally, on inputs of size
n
, the proof length is

\displaystyle\ell_{\pi}(n)=2^{r(n)}=T(n)\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(T(n)),

where
r(n)
is the randomness complexity of the PCPP verifier.

•

We write
\ell_{1}\coloneqq\ell_{V,A}(n)
and
\ell_{2}\coloneqq\ell_{\pi}(n)
.

Next, we give the protocol of the answer reduced verifier
V^{AR}
, which requires the provers to encode their proof
\pi
by
the Hadamard code of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell_{2}}
.
The protocol is very similar to the protocol presented in
[
NW19
, Figure 15]
, but we include it for completeness.

The answer reduced verifier
V^{AR}

Setup:
Flip one unbiased coin
\boldsymbol{b}\sim\left\{0,1\right\}
. Sample questions
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\sim\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}(\mathsf{input})
.
Sample a view
\boldsymbol{I}_{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{1},\boldsymbol{J}\sim V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}(\mathsf{input},\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})
.
Set
\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime}=\mu_{\ell_{2}}(\boldsymbol{J})
.
Randomly select
\boldsymbol{I}_{0}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{I}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq[2^{\ell_{1}}]
and
\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq[2^{\ell_{2}}]
such that

\lvert\boldsymbol{I}_{0}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\boldsymbol{I}_{1}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime\prime}\rvert=\kappa
, which is a sufficiently large constant.
Details about how to choose
\kappa
can be found in the proof below.
Set
\boldsymbol{T}_{0}=\boldsymbol{I}_{0}\cup\boldsymbol{I}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{T}_{1}=\boldsymbol{I}_{1}\cup\boldsymbol{I}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime}\cup\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime\prime}
.

With probability
1/10
each, perform one of the following ten tests

8
8

8

There are two answer cross-checks, one for
c=0
and one for
c=1
, similarly for the
answer consistency checks and answer code checks.
.

Verify

: Distribute the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{T}_{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{2}
.

Accept if
V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}(\mathsf{input},\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})
accepts on
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}|_{\boldsymbol{I}_{0}}
,
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}|_{\boldsymbol{I}_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{2}|_{\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime}}
.

Cross check

:

Consistency test:

Distribute the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{T}_{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{2}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{T}_{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{2}

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}=\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{2}=\boldsymbol{a}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Answer cross-check:

For
c=0,1
, distributed the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{T}_{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{2}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{c},\boldsymbol{T}_{c}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{c}

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{c}=\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{c}
.

Answer consistency check:

For
c=0,1
, distributed the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{c},\boldsymbol{T}_{c}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{c}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{c},\boldsymbol{T}_{c}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{c}

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{c}=\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{c}
.

Proof cross-check:

Distribute the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{T}_{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{2}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{U}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{2}

Accept if
\boldsymbol{a}_{2}=\boldsymbol{a}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Code checks

:

Answer code check:

For
c=0,1
,
sample questions
(\boldsymbol{w}_{0},\boldsymbol{w}_{1})\sim G_{\ell_{1}}(\boldsymbol{T}_{c})
.
Distributed the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{c},\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
\boldsymbol{x}_{c},\boldsymbol{w}_{1}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}
.

Accept if
G_{\ell_{1}}(\boldsymbol{T}_{c})
accepts on
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}
.

Proof code check:

Sample questions
(\boldsymbol{w}_{0},\boldsymbol{w}_{1})\sim G_{\ell_{2}}(\boldsymbol{U})
.
Distribute the questions as follows:

•

Player
\boldsymbol{b}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}_{0}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}
.

•

Player
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}}
: give
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}_{1}
; receive
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}
.

Accept if
G_{\ell_{2}}(\boldsymbol{U})
accepts on
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}
.

Figure 5:
The answer reduced verifier
V^{AR}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
be an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
protocol for a language
L
,
with question length
\ell_{V,Q}
, answer length
\ell_{V,A}
, sampling time
t_{V,Q}

and verification time
t_{V,A}
.
Suppose the
\mathrm{PCPP}
verifier is chosen so that
\gamma\leq 1/4
.
Suppose further that
V
has the following property: for any
\mathsf{input}\in L
, the prover has a
real commuting symmetric EPR strategy with a value
1
.
Then
V^{AR}
obtained by applying the the answer reduction procedure to
V
as shown in
Fig.

5
is also an

\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
verifier for
L
with the following two conditions:

Question length.

The new question length is
\ell_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=O(\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n))
.

Answer length.

The new answer length is
\ell_{V^{AR},A}(n)=O(1)
.

Sampling time.

The new question sampling time is

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\log_{2}(2^{\ell_{V,A}(n)}),\log_{2}(T(n)))+O(\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n)).

Verification time.

The new verification time is
t_{V^{AR},A}=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\ell_{1}(n),\log_{2}(T(n)))
.

Completeness.

If
\mathsf{input}\in L
, there is a value-
1
strategy for
V^{AR}
.

Soundness.

Given
\mathsf{input}
, suppose there is a strategy for
V^{AR}
with value
1-\varepsilon
.
Then there exists constants
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
such that there is a strategy for
V
on
\mathsf{input}
with
value
1-K_{1}-K_{2}\varepsilon^{1/128}
.

Efficient computability.

There exists an algorithm that takes the description of
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
as input and outputs the description of
V^{AR}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime},\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime})
in time
O(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)
.
Moreover,
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+O(1)
and
\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert+O(1)
.

Proof.

Question length
. The question of
V^{AR}
consists of a question from
V
and queries to the encodings of the answers and the
\mathrm{PCPP}
proof. Hence,

\displaystyle\ell_{V^{AR},Q}(n)\leq 2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+2(\kappa+q(n))\cdot\ell_{1}(n)+(\kappa+q(n))\cdot\ell_{2}(n)=O(\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n)),

where
q(n)
is the query complexity of the PCPP verifier.

Answer length
. The prover only needs to answer the queries, so the answer consists of at most
3(\kappa+q(n))
bits.

Sampling time
. The sampling algorithm of the answer-reduced protocol needs to run the sampling algorithm of the
\mathrm{PCPP}
verifier, which takes time

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{PCPP},Q}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\log_{2}(2^{\ell_{V,A}(n)}),\log_{2}(T(n))).

The sampling algorithm must also run
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
, the embedding algorithm
\mu_{\ell_{2}(n)}
, and sample random indices in the encodings.
Hence, the sampling time is

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},Q}(n)

\displaystyle\leq t_{V,Q}(n)+t_{\mathrm{PCPP},Q}(n)+q(n)\cdot\ell_{2}(n)+2\kappa\ell_{1}(n)+\kappa\ell_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\log_{2}(2^{\ell_{V,A}(n)}),\log_{2}(T(n)))+O(\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n)),

where
q(n)=O(1)
is the query complexity of the
\mathrm{PCPP}
verifier.

Verification time
. Since the verification time of the code checks and consistency tests are
O(1)
,

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},A}(n)\leq t_{\mathrm{PCPP},A}(n)+O(1)=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V,Q}(n),\ell_{1}(n),\log_{2}(T(n))).

Efficient computatbility.
This follows from the observation that the descriptions of
\mathsf{Alg}^{\prime}_{Q}
and
\mathsf{Alg}^{\prime}_{A}

contains both
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
and
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
respectively with new instructions.
Since the new instructions added to
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
and
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
are independent of
\mathsf{input}
,
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
and
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
, the time to write them down are
O(1)
, and their sizes are also
O(1)
.

Completeness
. This follows the same proof of the completeness part of
[
NW19
, Theorem 17.10]
.
If an honest prover gets one question
x_{b}
, the prover will compute its answer, encode its answer using the Hadamard code, and answer the queries accordingly.
If an honest prover gets both questions, the prover will compute its answers, compute a
\mathrm{PCPP}
proof to certify these answers are correct, compute the Hadamard encodings of the answers and the proof, and answer the queries accordingly.

Soundness
. The constant
K_{1}
depends on the parameter
\kappa=\lvert\boldsymbol{I}_{0}^{\prime}\rvert
, so we should set
\kappa
to be a sufficiently large constant so that
1-K_{1}-K_{2}\varepsilon^{1/128}
is greater than the soundness
s
of
V
.
Operationally, the views are augmented by
\kappa
uniformly randomly chosen coordinates. The purpose of this is to drive the distance of the Hadamard code up from
1/2
to
1-1/2^{\kappa}
, which will be needed for
Lemma

2.37
.

Let
C_{I}=\lvert\boldsymbol{I}_{0}\rvert+\kappa=\lvert\boldsymbol{I}_{1}\rvert+\kappa
and
C_{J}=\lvert\boldsymbol{J}\rvert+\kappa
be two constants.
Suppose
\mathsf{input}
is not in
L
. Let
(\left|\psi\right\rangle,M)
be a strategy that
passes with probability
1-\varepsilon
.
This strategy can pass each
Answer code check
with probability
1-10\varepsilon
. Given values
c
and
x_{c}
,
write
1-\varepsilon_{c,x_{c}}
for the probability the code check passes conditioned on these values.
Then with probability at least
1-10\varepsilon^{1/2}
,
\varepsilon_{c,x_{c}}\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}
.
When this occurs, we can apply
6.5
to
G_{\ell_{1}}(\boldsymbol{T}_{c})
where the distribution of

\boldsymbol{T}_{c}
denoted by
D_{x_{c}}
is determined by
c
and
x_{c}
.

6.5
implies that there exists Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{x_{c}}
,
\left|aux_{x_{c}}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{x_{c}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{x_{c}}

and projective measurement
\left\{G^{x_{c}}_{u}\right\}
on
\mathcal{H}^{x_{c}}
such that

\displaystyle\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(M^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{A,x_{c}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes G^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{x_{c}}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(C_{I}^{3}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{c,x_{c}}})

where
\mathds{1}_{A}=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A,x_{c}}}
and similar for
\mathds{1}_{B}
.
When this does not occur, we can still assume such Hilbert spaces and projective measurements so that

\displaystyle\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(M^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{A,x_{c}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes G^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{x_{c}}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(1).

When averaging over
c
and
x_{c}
,

\displaystyle\bigE_{c,x_{c}}\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(M^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{A,x_{c}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes G^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{x_{c}}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(C_{I}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

Passing the
Proof code check
implies that there exists Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}
, states
\left|aux_{x_{0},x_{1}}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}

and projective measurements
\left\{H^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{w}\right\}
on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}
such that

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\bigE_{U\sim D_{(x_{0},x_{1})}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{J}}}\lVert(M^{x_{0},x_{1},U}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{A,x_{0},x_{1}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes H^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{[w|_{U}=a]})\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{x_{0},x_{1}}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(C_{J}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

The next step is ensuring the
G
and
H
measurements act on the same Hilbert space.
Let

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle=\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes(\otimes_{x}\left|aux_{x}\right\rangle)\otimes(\otimes_{x_{0},x_{1}}\left|aux_{x_{0},x_{1}}\right\rangle)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{x_{c}}_{u}=G^{x_{c}}_{u}\otimes(\otimes_{x\neq x_{c}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x}})\otimes(\otimes_{x_{0},x_{1}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}})

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u}=H^{x_{0},x_{1}}\otimes(\otimes_{x}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x}})\otimes(\otimes_{(z_{0},z_{1})\neq(x_{0},x_{1})}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{z_{0},z_{1}}}),

and, let

\displaystyle N^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a_{c}}=M^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a_{c}}\otimes(\otimes_{x}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x}})\otimes(\otimes_{x_{0},x_{1}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}})

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},U}=M^{x_{0},x_{1},U}_{a_{2}}\otimes(\otimes_{x}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x}})\otimes(\otimes_{x_{0},x_{1}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}})

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}}=M^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}}\otimes(\otimes_{x}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x}})\otimes(\otimes_{x_{0},x_{1}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{x_{0},x_{1}}}).

Note that we omit the permutation of the Hilbert spaces in the definitions above.
Then for all
x_{c}

\displaystyle\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(N^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(M^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{A,x_{c}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes G^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\psi\right\rangle\otimes\left|aux_{x_{c}}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle\bigE_{c,x_{c}}\bigE_{T_{c}\sim D_{x_{c}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{I}}}\lVert(N^{x_{c},T_{c}}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{c}}_{[w|_{T_{c}}=a]})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(C_{I}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}),

(26)

and

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\bigE_{U\sim D_{(x_{0},x_{1})}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{C_{J}}}\lVert(N^{x_{0},x_{1},U}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\tilde{H}^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{[w|_{U}=a]})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq O(C_{J}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

(27)

Note these relations also hold with the two systems flipped.

Similarly, passing the
Cross Checks
implies that

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{0},T_{0}}_{a_{0}}

(28)

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{1}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{1},T_{1}}_{a_{1}}

(29)

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},T_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{0},T_{0}}_{a_{0}}

(30)

\displaystyle N^{x_{1},T_{1}}_{a_{1}}\otimes\mathds{1}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{1},T_{1}}_{a_{1}}

(31)

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{2}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{0},x_{1},U}_{a_{2}}

(32)

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\approx_{O(\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}},

(33)

with respect to
\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle
over the distribution
D
of
x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U
.
Note that here we use the
\approx
notation introduced at the beginning of
Section

2.5

to make the dependence on the distribution implicit.
These equations combined with
Eqs.

26
and
27
imply the
measurements
\left\{N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}}\right\}
,
\left\{\tilde{G}^{x}_{u}\right\}
and
\left\{\tilde{H}^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{w}\right\}
satisfy
conditions of
Lemma

2.37
with respect to
\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle
and distribution
D
.
Let

\displaystyle\left\{\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}:=\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u_{0}}\cdot\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{u_{1}}\cdot\tilde{H}^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{w}\cdot\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{u_{1}}\cdot\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u_{0}}\right\}

be a POVM constructed following
Lemma

2.37
.
Recall that
T_{c}
and
U
has
\kappa
independent coordinates, so two different codewords agree on
T_{c}
or
U
with a probability at most

\eta_{H}^{\kappa}=1/2^{\kappa}
.
Letting
C_{0}=\max\{C_{I},C_{J}\}
,
Hence we can applying
Lemma

2.37
to this POVM with
k=3
,
\delta:=C_{0}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}
and
\varepsilon:=1/2^{\kappa}
, and get that

\displaystyle N^{x_{0},x_{1},T_{0},T_{1},U}_{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\approx_{O(C_{0}^{3/16}\varepsilon^{1/64}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/8}})}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{[u_{0}|_{T_{0}},u_{1}|_{T_{1}},w|_{U}=a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}]}

(34)

with respect to
\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle
and
D
, where
[u_{0}|_{T_{0}},u_{1}|_{T_{1}},w|_{U}=a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}]
means that
\mathsf{Enc}_{\ell_{1}}(u_{0})|_{T_{0}}=a_{0}
and etc..
Passing
Verify
with a probability at least
1-10\varepsilon
along with

Equations

34
and
2.32
implies that
\left\{\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\right\}
can be used to pass the verify test with probability
1-10\varepsilon-O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}})
where we upper bound
C_{0}^{3/16}
by
C_{0}^{1/4}
. The player would measure
\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda
on
\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle
and return the local views of the measurement outcomes according to the questions.

Consider the measurements
\left\{\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1}}:=\sum_{w}\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\right\}

Let

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle:=\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{u_{0},u_{1}:V(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1})=1}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle,

which is the probability that measuring with
\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1}}
gives answers

u_{0}
and
u_{1}
accepted by the verifier
V
when the questions are
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{0},u_{1}:\\
V(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{w}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{0},u_{1}:\\
V(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{w}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\cdot\Pr_{R}[V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}^{u_{0},u_{1},w}(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},2\cdot 2^{\ell_{1}};R)=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr[(\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle,\Lambda)\text{ pass {verify} check }]

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{0},u_{1}:\\
V(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{w}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\cdot\Pr_{R}[V^{u_{0},u_{1},w}_{\mathrm{PCPP}}(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},2\cdot 2^{\ell_{1}};R)=1]

\displaystyle\geq 1-10\varepsilon-O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{0},u_{1}:\\
V(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{w}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{u_{0},u_{1},w}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\cdot\Pr_{R}[V^{u_{0},u_{1},w}_{\mathrm{PCPP}}(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},2\cdot 2^{\ell_{1}};R)=1]

\displaystyle\geq 1-10\varepsilon-O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}})-(1-p)s,

where
s
is the soundness of
V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}
.
In the derivation above,
\Pr_{R}[V^{u_{0},u_{1},w}_{\mathrm{PCPP}}(\mathsf{input},x_{0},x_{1},2\cdot 2^{\ell_{1}};R)=1]
is the probability that
V_{\mathrm{PCPP}}

accepts
\mathsf{input}
.
For any
x_{0},x_{1},u_{0},u_{1}
not accepted by
V
, this probability is below
s
by
[
NW19
, Proposition 17.8]
.
Hence

\displaystyle p\geq\frac{1-10\varepsilon-O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}})-s}{1-s}=1-\frac{10\varepsilon+O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}})}{1-s}.

In the end, we use
(\left\{\tilde{G}^{x}_{u}\right\},\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle)
as a strategy for
V
.
Applying
Lemma

2.35
to
Eqs.

30
,
31
and
26
, we get that

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}\otimes\mathds{1}\approx_{O(C_{0}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4})}\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}

with respect to the distribution of
x_{0}
and the distribution of
T_{0}
determined by
x_{0}
on the state
\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle
.
Since
\left\{\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\right\}
is a projective measurement, we know

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0}}\bigE_{T_{0}\sim D_{x_{0}}}\sum_{a}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\geq 1-O(C_{0}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

On the other hand

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0}}\bigE_{T_{0}\sim D_{x_{0}}}\sum_{a}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u|_{T_{0}}=a}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{u}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle+\bigE_{x_{0}}\bigE_{T_{0}\sim D_{x_{0}}}\sum_{u\neq u^{\prime}:u|_{T_{0}}=u^{\prime}|_{T_{0}}}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u^{\prime}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{u}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle+\bigE_{x_{0}}\bigE_{T_{0}\sim D_{x_{0}}}\sum_{u\neq u^{\prime}}\mathds{1}[u|_{T_{0}}=u^{\prime}|_{T_{0}}]\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u^{\prime}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle.

Since for all
x_{0}
and
u\neq u^{\prime}
,
\bigE_{T_{0}\sim D_{x_{0}}}\mathds{1}[u|_{T_{0}}=u^{\prime}|_{T_{0}}]\leq 1/2^{\kappa}
, we know

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{u}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\geq 1-1/2^{\kappa}-O(C_{0}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

Again, because
\left\{\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\right\}
is a projective measurement

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{u}\lVert(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{u})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\kappa-1}}+O(C_{0}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/4}).

Let
S(x_{0},x_{1})=\left\{(a_{0},a_{1})\mid V(x_{0},x_{1},a_{0},a_{1})=1\right\}
.
We can calculate

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle-\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\lVert\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})(\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}})(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq 1\cdot\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{a_{0}}\lVert(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq O(\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/2}}+C_{0}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{1/8}),

and

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle-\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\lVert\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})(\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}})(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq 1\cdot\sqrt{\bigE_{x_{0}}\sum_{a_{0}}\lVert(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\mathds{1}-\mathds{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq O(\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/2}}+C_{0}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{1/8}).

Note that
\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}=\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{a_{0},a_{1}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lvert\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|(\tilde{G}^{x_{0}}_{a_{0}}\otimes\tilde{G}^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}-\mathds{1}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{a_{0},a_{1}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle\rvert\leq O(\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/2}}+C_{0}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{1/8}).

On the other hand, we have shown

\displaystyle\bigE_{x_{0},x_{1}}\sum_{(a_{0},a_{1})\in S}\left\langle\tilde{\psi}\right|\mathds{1}\otimes\Lambda^{x_{0},x_{1}}_{a_{0},a_{1}})\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle=p\geq 1-O(C_{0}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{1/128}+\frac{1}{2^{\kappa/16}}).

Since the big-O notations in the derivations above hide constants that are independent of
\kappa
,
the winning probability of the strategy
(\left\{\tilde{G}^{x}_{u}\right\},\left|\tilde{\psi}\right\rangle)
for
V

is at least
1-\frac{C_{1}}{2^{\kappa/16}}-C_{2}C_{0}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{1/128}
for some constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.
Hence,
K_{1}=\frac{C_{1}}{2^{\kappa/16}}
and
K_{2}=C_{2}C_{0}^{3/2}
in the soundness statement.
We need to pick
\kappa
large enough such that
1-\frac{C_{1}}{2^{\kappa/16}}>s
, then we can solve for

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\Big[\frac{1-\frac{C_{1}}{2^{\kappa/16}}-s}{C_{2}C_{0}^{3/2}}\Big]^{128},

such that
1-\varepsilon_{0}
is the soundness of
V^{AR}
.
∎

6.4
Putting everything together

Theorem 6.10
.

\mathrm{RE}
is contained in
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}[\operatorname{poly},O(1)]
with completeness
1
and a constant soundness.

Proof.

We know that there is an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}[\operatorname{poly},\operatorname{poly}]
protocol
V=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q},\mathsf{Alg}_{A})
for any language in
\mathrm{RE}
. To prove the theorem, we construct an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}[\operatorname{poly},O(1)]
protocol for the same language, by applying several answer reduction transformations to
V
. Specifically, we apply two iterations of an answer reduction scheme based on the low-degree test over finite fields to reduce the answer size to
O(\operatorname{poly}\log\log(n))
, followed by one iteration of answer reduction based on the Hadamard code over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
to further reduce the answer size to
O(1)
. The effect of each step is summarized in the following table.

Question

size

Answer

size

Sampling

time

Verification

time

Decision

complexity

Original protocol

poly(n)

poly(n)

poly(n)

poly(n)

poly(n)

After Step 1

(
Theorem

6.2
)

poly(n)

polylog(n)

poly(n)

poly(n)

polylog(n)

After Step 2

(
Theorem

6.2
)

poly(n)

polyloglog(n)

poly(n)

poly(n)

polyloglog(n)

After Step 3

(
Theorem

6.9
)

poly(n)

O(1)

poly(n)

poly(n)

O(1)

Table 1:
Effect of each step of the proof

Step 1.
We parallel repeat and oracularize
V
to ensure its soundness is at most
1/2
, and apply the answer reduction technique summarized in
Theorem

6.2
.
Since parallel repetition and oracularization only introduce constant overhead in the question length, answer length, sampling time and verification time, we still use
V
to denote the oracularized protocol.
Denote the answer-reduced protocol by
V_{1}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{1}},\mathsf{Alg}_{A_{1}})
.
Since
d_{V,A}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(n)
and
k(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))=\operatorname{polylog}(n)
,
by
Theorem

6.2
, the new question length is

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{1},Q}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))\cdot(2\ell_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

The new answer length and decision complexity are

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{1},A}(n)=d_{V_{1},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))=\operatorname{polylog}(n).

The new sampling time is

\displaystyle t_{V_{1},Q}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))\cdot(t_{V,Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

The new verification time is

\displaystyle t_{V_{1},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n))\cdot(t_{V,Q}(n)+t_{V,A}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V,A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Lastly,
V
has completeness 1 and soundness at most
1/2
, so is
V_{1}
.

Step 2.
We apply
Theorem

6.2
again and denote the new protocol by
V_{2}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{2}},\mathsf{Alg}_{A_{2}})
.
Since
d_{V_{1},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(n)
and
k(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))=\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)
,
by
Theorem

6.2
, the new question length is

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{2},Q}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))\cdot(2\ell_{V_{1},Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

The new answer length and decision complexity are

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{2},A}(n)=d_{V_{2},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))=\operatorname{polyloglog}(n).

The new sampling time is

\displaystyle t_{V_{2},Q}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))\cdot(t_{V_{1},Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

The new verification time is

\displaystyle t_{V_{2},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n))\cdot(t_{V_{1},Q}(n)+t_{V_{1},A}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{1},A}(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Moreover,
V_{2}
has perfect completeness and soundness at most
1/2
.

Step 3.

The protocol
V_{2}
is oracularized again.
Again, since oracularization only introduces constant overhead in the question length, answer length, sampling time and verification time, we still use
V_{2}
to denote the oracularized protocol.
Define the language
L_{\mathsf{Enc}}
as in
Definition

6.7
for
V_{2}
.
We can calculate the parameters in
Definition

6.8
.
First,
L_{\mathsf{Enc}}\in\mathrm{DTIME}(T(n))
where

\displaystyle T(n)=t_{V_{2},A}(n)+2^{\ell_{V_{2},A}(n)}=\operatorname{poly}(n),

where the decoder

\mathsf{Dec}_{\ell_{V_{2},A}(n)}
takes
O(2^{\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)})
time.
Moreover, the query lengths are

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(n)=\ell_{V_{2},A}(n)=\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)

\displaystyle\ell_{2}(n)=\ell_{\pi}(n)=T(n)\log_{2}(T(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Then the PCPP question sampling and verification times are

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{PCPP},Q}(n)=t_{V_{2},Q}(n)+\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V_{2},Q}(n),\ell_{V_{2},A}(n),\log_{2}(T(n)))+O(\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n),

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{PCPP},A}(n)=\operatorname{poly}(n+\ell_{V_{2},Q}(n),\ell_{V_{2},A}(n),\log_{2}(T(n)))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Next, we apply the answer reduction technique in
Theorem

6.9
to get verifier
V^{AR}
.
By
Theorem

6.9
,

\displaystyle\ell_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=O(\ell_{V_{2},Q}(n)+\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n),

\displaystyle\ell_{V^{AR},A}(n)=O(1),

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},Q}(n)=O(t_{V_{2},Q}(n)+\ell_{1}(n)+\ell_{2}(n)+t_{\mathrm{PCPP},Q}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n),

\displaystyle t_{V^{AR},A}(n)=O(t_{\mathrm{PCPP},A}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Moreover, it is easy to see that the new decision complexity is
d_{V^{AR},A}(n)=O(1)
.
The perfect completeness and constant soundness of
V^{AR}
follow from
Theorem

6.9
.

Alternatively, we can apply the answer reduction technique of
Theorem

6.2
iteratively until the answer size is constant.
The proof follows the same line of argument in the proof of
[
NZ23
, Theorem 54]
, so we only sketch the proof here.
The sampler
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
and decider
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
both start by calculating the description of
V_{0}=(\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{0}},\mathsf{Alg}_{A_{0}})
, which is an
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}[\operatorname{poly},\operatorname{poly}]
protocol for
\mathrm{RE}
, then repeatedly applying the answer reduction procedure from
Theorem

6.2
followed by parallel repetition and oracularization to calculate the description of
V_{i+1}

from the description of
V_{i}
for
i\geq 0
until
V_{m}
has answer size
O(1)
.
Then
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}
executes
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{m}}
to sample the questions.
When the answers are returned, the decider
\mathsf{Alg}_{A}
executes
\mathsf{Alg}_{A_{m}}
to check the answers.

Following the same analysis, we can get
m=O(\log\log\log(\ell_{V,A}(n)))
.
Besides the
O(1)
answer size and decision complexity, the question size, sampling time, and verification time of
V_{m}
are:

Question size.
The question size follows the recursive relation

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{i+1},Q}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))(2\ell_{V_{i},Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))).

Since
d_{V_{i+1},A}(n)=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))
, we can bound

\displaystyle\ell_{V_{m},Q}(n)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n))(2\ell_{V_{m-1},Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n)))

\displaystyle=2^{m}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))]\cdot\ell_{V_{0},Q}(n)+\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}2^{m-1-i}\prod_{j=i}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{j},A}(n))]\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m}\operatorname{polylog}(n)\ell_{V_{0},Q}(n)+2^{m}m\operatorname{polylog}(n)\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{0},A}(n)))

\displaystyle=O(\operatorname{polylog}(n)\operatorname{poly}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(n))=O(\operatorname{poly}(n)),

where we upper bound
\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))]=\operatorname{polylog}(n)\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)\ldots O(1)
by

\operatorname{polylog}(n)\cdot\operatorname{polyloglog}(n)^{m}=\operatorname{polylog}(n)
.

Sampling time.
It takes
m
iterations for the sampler to calculate
V_{m}
.
The
(i+1)
th iteration takes time
O(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{i}}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A_{i}}\rvert)=O(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert+i)
.
Hence, the total computation time is
O(m(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)+m^{2})
.
The running time of
\mathsf{Alg}_{Q_{m}}
follows the relation

\displaystyle t_{V_{m},Q}(n)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n))\cdot(t_{V_{m-1},Q}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n)))

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))]\cdot t_{V_{0},Q}(n)+\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\prod_{j=i}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{j},A}(n))]\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{polylog}(n)t_{V_{0},Q}(n)+m\operatorname{polylog}(n)\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{0},A}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Hence, the total sampling time of
V_{m}
is
O(m(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)+m^{2}+\operatorname{poly}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n)
.

Verification time.
Similar to the previous case, the time to calculate
V_{m}
is

O(m(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)+m^{2})
.
The verification time of
V_{m}
is

\displaystyle t_{V_{m},A}(n)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n))(t_{V_{m-1},Q}(n)+t_{V_{m-1},A}(n)+\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{m-1},A}(n)))

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))]\cdot(t_{V_{0},Q}(n)+t_{V_{0},A}(n))+\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\prod_{j=i}^{m-1}[\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{j},A}(n))]\cdot\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{i},A}(n))

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{polylog}(n)(t_{V_{0},Q}(n)+t_{V_{0},A}(n))+m\operatorname{polylog}(n)\operatorname{polylog}(d_{V_{0},A}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n).

Hence the total verification time is
O(m(\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{Q}\rvert+\lvert\mathsf{Alg}_{A}\rvert)+m^{2}+\operatorname{poly}(n))=\operatorname{poly}(n)
.
Lastly, the protocol
V_{m}
has completeness 1 and soundness at most
1/2
.

∎

Remark 6.11
.

We have just shown how to use our new answer reduction techniques to get very small answer sizes, without a large overhead in question length. A natural question that arises is whether this can be applied to the protocol of
[
NZ23
]
, which has
constant
question length but polylogarithmic answer length, in order to obtain a protocol with total communication that scales as
O(\operatorname{poly}\log\log(n))
. This would contradict the lower bound in
[
NZ23
]
, which shows that
\mathrm{RE}
(or indeed
\mathrm{EEXP}
) cannot be decided by
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
protocols with total communication smaller than
\log(n)
. Indeed, our tighter answer reduction fails to give such a result when applied to the constant-question-size protocol of
[
NZ23
]
. This is because of the phenomenon described in
Remark

6.3
: an application of question reduction resets the decision complexity to be
\operatorname{poly}(n)
, so in particular, the protocol from that work has decision complexity
\operatorname{poly}(n)
, and applying answer reduction to it would blow up both the question size and answer size to
\operatorname{poly}\log(n)
.

Appendix A
Lemmas for Noisy
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}

Smoothing.

The following lemma reduces the degrees of the POVMs of an
\mathrm{MIP}^{\ast}
strategy.

Lemma A.1
.

[
QY21
, Lemma 6.1]
9
9

9

The statement is slightly different from that in
[
QY21
, Lemma 6.1]
. The difference arises due to our relocation of the truncating step, which was in
[
QY21
, Lemma 10.5]
.

Given parameters
0\leq\rho<1
,
0<\delta<1
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
m\geq 2
, and an
m
-dimensional noisy MES
\psi_{AB}
with the quantum maximal correlation
\rho=\rho\left(\psi_{AB}\right)
, there exists
d=d\left(\rho,\delta\right)
and a map
f:\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n},
such that for any positive semi-definite matrices
P,Q\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
satisfying
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1
. The matrices
P^{(1)}=f\left(P\right)
and
Q^{(1)}=f\left(Q\right)
satisfy that

1.

P^{(1)}
and
Q^{(1)}
are of degree at most
d
.

2.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{(1)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1~\mbox{and}~{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{(1)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1.

3.

\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{(1)}\otimes Q^{(1)}\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\psi^{\otimes n}_{AB}\right)\right|\leq\delta.

4.

\frac{1}{m^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(P^{(1)})\leq\delta
and
\frac{1}{m^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(Q^{(1)})\leq\delta.

5.

the map
f
is linear and unital.

In particular, we can take
d=\frac{C\log^{2}\frac{1}{\delta}}{\delta(1-\rho)}
for some absolute constant
C
.

Remark A.2
.

It is easily verified that for the above lemma,
for each
\sigma\in[m^{2}]^{n}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\lvert\widehat{P}^{(1)}(\sigma)\rvert\leq\lvert\widehat{P}(\sigma)\rvert~\mbox{and}~\lvert\widehat{Q}^{(1)}(\sigma)\rvert\leq\lvert\widehat{Q}(\sigma)\rvert.

This is because in fact
f
applies depolarizing noise on
P
and then eliminates the high degree parts. So the Fourier coefficients are non-increasing in absolute value.

Regularization.

The following lemma allows us to identify high-influence registers,
and the number of such registers can be upper-bounded.

Lemma A.3
.

[
QY21
, Lemma 7.4]

Given
0<\tau<1
,
d,n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
m\geq 2
, and a degree-
d
matrix
P\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
satisfying
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq 1
, there exists a subset
H\subseteq[n]
of size
h=\lvert H\rvert\leq\frac{d}{\tau}
such that for any
i\notin H
,

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P^{\leq d}\right)\leq\tau.

Rounding.

The following lemma shows that we can round a given set of matrices that sum up to
\mathds{1}

to a close-by POVM.

Lemma A.4
.

Given
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{X}}\in\left(\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}\right)^{t}
satisfying that
\sum_{i=1}^{t}X_{i}=\mathds{1}
, define

\mathcal{R}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{X}}\right)=\arg\min\left\{{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{X}}-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{P}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}:\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{P}}~\mbox{is a POVM}\right\}

It holds that

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{R}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{X}}\right)-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{X}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{3(t+1)}{m^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(X_{i})+6\left(\frac{t}{m^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta(X_{i})\right)^{1/2}.

Miscellaneous Lemmas.

The following lemmas are used throughout
Appendix

C
.

Fact A.5
.

[
QY21
, Fact 2.1]

Given registers
A,B
, operators
P\in\mathcal{H}\left(A\right),Q\in\mathcal{H}\left(B\right)
and a bipartite state
\psi_{AB}
, it holds that

\lvert\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\psi_{AB}\right)\rvert\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}\,P^{2}\psi_{A}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Tr}\,Q^{2}\psi_{B}\right)^{1/2}.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\}^{x\in\mathcal{X}}_{a\in\mathcal{A}},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}^{y\in\mathcal{Y}}_{b\in\mathcal{B}}
,
\left\{\tilde{P}^{\,x}_{a}\right\}^{x\in\mathcal{X}}_{a\in\mathcal{A}},\left\{\tilde{Q}^{\,y}_{b}\right\}^{y\in\mathcal{Y}}_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
be four sets of matrices. If for all
\left(x,y,a,b\right)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
,

\lvert\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{\,x}_{a}\otimes Q^{\,y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\tilde{P}^{\,x}_{a}\otimes\tilde{Q}^{\,y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)\rvert\leq\delta{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{\,x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{\,y}_{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}

for some
\delta>0
.
Then

\left|\mathrm{val}_{n}\left(\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)-\mathrm{val}_{n}\left(\left\{\tilde{P}^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{\tilde{Q}^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)\right|\leq\delta t\left(\sum_{x,a}\mu_{A}(x){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{\,x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{y,b}\mu_{B}(y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{\,y}_{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{val}_{n}\left(\left\{P^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{Q^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)-\mathrm{val}_{n}\left(\left\{\tilde{P}^{\,x}_{a}\right\},\left\{\tilde{Q}^{\,y}_{b}\right\}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y)\lvert\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{\,x}_{a}\otimes Q^{\,y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\tilde{P}^{\,x}_{a}\otimes\tilde{Q}^{\,y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\delta\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{\,x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{\,y}_{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\delta\left(\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P^{\,x}_{a}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{x,y,a,b}\mu(x,y){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Q^{\,y}_{b}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\quad\mbox{(Cauchy Schwarz)}

\displaystyle=~
